# SECTION: Endocrine System (Count: 140 Questions)


### Question 141
A nurse is educating a newly diagnosed client about the foundational differences between Type 1 and Type 2 diabetes mellitus. Which statement by the nurse is accurate to include in the teaching?

A) "Type 1 diabetes is primarily treated with lifestyle changes and oral medications."
B) "Type 2 diabetes always develops during childhood, while Type 1 develops in later adulthood."
C) "In Type 2 diabetes, the pancreas produces toxic insulin that attacks the body's healthy cells."
D) "In Type 1 diabetes, the body does not produce insulin, making lifelong insulin therapy necessary."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Client Teaching
**System Focus:** Type 1 vs Type 2 basics

**Rationale:** Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune condition where the beta cells of the pancreas are destroyed, resulting in an absolute lack of insulin. Therefore, lifelong exogenous insulin therapy is required. Option A is incorrect because Type 1 requires insulin, not just lifestyle changes or oral meds. Option B is incorrect as Type 1 is more commonly diagnosed in childhood/youth (though it can occur at any age), and Type 2 is typically associated with adults (though pediatric cases are rising). Option C is incorrect; Type 2 involves insulin resistance and a relative insulin deficiency, not "toxic" insulin.
</details>

---

### Question 142
A nurse is treating a conscious client who has a capillary blood glucose reading of 60 mg/dL. What is the correct procedure for managing this client's mild hypoglycemia?

A) Inject 1 mg of glucagon intramuscularly into the deltoid.
B) Administer a rapid-acting insulin bolus based on a sliding scale.
C) Provide 15 grams of a fast-acting carbohydrate, such as half a cup of fruit juice.
D) Give the client a heavy meal rich in proteins and complex fats immediately.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Procedural Step
**System Focus:** Hypoglycemia

**Rationale:** The standard procedural step for treating mild hypoglycemia (blood glucose < 70 mg/dL) in a conscious client who can swallow safely is the "Rule of 15." This involves giving 15 grams of a fast-acting, simple carbohydrate (like 4 oz of juice or regular soda) and rechecking the blood glucose in 15 minutes. Option A is reserved for unconscious clients or those unable to swallow. Option B would severely worsen the hypoglycemia. Option D is incorrect because fats and proteins delay the absorption of carbohydrates, which prevents the rapid correction of the low blood sugar.
</details>

---

### Question 143
A client has been taking levothyroxine for hypothyroidism for the past three months. Which assessment finding indicates to the nurse that the medication is having the expected therapeutic effect?

A) The client reports having more energy and no longer feeling constantly fatigued.
B) The client's resting heart rate is consistently reading 118 beats per minute.
C) The client states they are constantly feeling cold and wearing extra layers of clothing.
D) The client has experienced an unintentional 10-pound weight gain over the last month.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Expected vs Unexpected
**System Focus:** Thyroid medication

**Rationale:** Levothyroxine is a synthetic thyroid hormone used to treat hypothyroidism. Expected therapeutic effects include a reversal of hypothyroid symptoms, such as an increase in energy levels, normalized heart rate, and stabilized weight. Option A represents an expected positive outcome. Option B (tachycardia) is an unexpected, adverse effect indicating the dose may be too high (hyperthyroidism). Options C (cold intolerance) and D (weight gain) are signs of continuing hypothyroidism, indicating the medication is not yet effective or the dose is too low.
</details>

---

### Question 144
A nurse is reviewing morning vital signs and capillary blood glucose results for a client with Type 1 diabetes. The client is currently experiencing tachycardia, pallor, diaphoresis (sweating), and irritability. Which data point explains these clinical findings?

A) Hemoglobin A1c of 6.2%
B) Capillary blood glucose of 185 mg/dL
C) Capillary blood glucose of 52 mg/dL
D) Fasting blood glucose of 110 mg/dL

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Data Interpretation
**System Focus:** Hypoglycemia

**Rationale:** Tachycardia, pallor, diaphoresis, and irritability are classic sympathetic nervous system responses to hypoglycemia (low blood sugar). A blood glucose level of 52 mg/dL directly correlates with and explains these symptoms. Option A is a well-controlled Hemoglobin A1c level. Option B represents hyperglycemia, which would present with different symptoms (e.g., polyuria, polydipsia). Option D is a normal fasting blood glucose level. 
</details>

---

### Question 145
A nurse is taking a health history from a client with Type 2 diabetes who is prescribed an oral sulfonylurea medication. Which habit reported by the client places them at the highest risk for a hypoglycemic event?

A) Drinking 64 ounces of plain water daily
B) Skipping meals when feeling too busy to eat
C) Walking for 20 minutes at a moderate pace after dinner
D) Checking capillary blood glucose levels every morning

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Risk Recognition
**System Focus:** Hypoglycemia

**Rationale:** Sulfonylureas work by stimulating the pancreas to release more insulin regardless of the client's current blood glucose level. Therefore, if a client takes this medication and skips meals (Option B), there will not be enough glucose in the bloodstream to match the circulating insulin, leading to a high risk of hypoglycemia. Options A, C, and D are healthy, highly recommended behaviors for a client with Type 2 diabetes and do not place the client at high risk for a hypoglycemic event.
</details>
### Question 146
A client newly diagnosed with Type 1 diabetes asks the nurse, "Why do I have to take insulin shots? My neighbor has diabetes and just takes a pill." Which response by the nurse is best?

A) "Pills are only effective for clients who are over the age of 65."
B) "You will eventually transition to pills once your blood sugar stabilizes."
C) "In Type 1 diabetes, your body makes no insulin, so it must be replaced by injection."
D) "Oral diabetic medications are too strong for someone who is newly diagnosed."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Client Teaching

**Rationale:** Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune condition characterized by the absolute destruction of pancreatic beta cells, meaning the body produces zero insulin. Because of this, exogenous (injected) insulin is required for life. Type 2 diabetes typically involves insulin resistance and decreased production, which can often be managed with oral medications (pills) that help the body use its remaining insulin more effectively. Options A, B, and D are factually incorrect regarding the pathophysiology of Type 1 diabetes.
</details>

---

### Question 147
A nurse finds a conscious client with Type 1 diabetes who is shaking, sweating, and reports feeling very hungry. A capillary blood glucose check reveals a level of 58 mg/dL. What is the nurse's immediate action?

A) Administer 1 mg of glucagon intramuscularly.
B) Provide 15 grams of a fast-acting carbohydrate.
C) Give the client a high-protein snack, such as cheese and crackers.
D) Wait 15 minutes, recheck the blood glucose, and then intervene.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Procedural Step

**Rationale:** For a conscious client experiencing hypoglycemia (blood glucose < 70 mg/dL), the standard procedural step is the "Rule of 15": provide 15 grams of fast-acting carbohydrates (e.g., 4 oz of fruit juice or regular soda), wait 15 minutes, and recheck the blood glucose. Glucagon (Option A) is reserved for unconscious clients or those unable to swallow. Protein (Option C) does not raise blood glucose quickly enough for an acute hypoglycemic episode. Waiting 15 minutes without intervening first (Option D) could lead to worsening hypoglycemia, loss of consciousness, or seizures.
</details>

---

### Question 148
A client has been taking levothyroxine for hypothyroidism for one month. During a clinic visit, the client reports feeling "jittery," having a racing heart, and experiencing unintentional weight loss. How should the nurse interpret these findings?

A) The client needs to take the medication with a full meal to prevent side effects.
B) The client is developing a normal physiological tolerance to the medication.
C) These are expected signs that the medication is working properly.
D) These are unexpected signs indicating the medication dose may be too high.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Expected vs Unexpected

**Rationale:** Levothyroxine is a synthetic thyroid hormone used to treat hypothyroidism. Signs such as tachycardia ("racing heart"), tremors ("jittery"), and weight loss are unexpected adverse effects indicating hyperthyroidism. This means the prescribed dose is likely too high and needs to be evaluated by the healthcare provider. Levothyroxine should be taken on an empty stomach, not with a meal (Option A). Options B and C are incorrect as these symptoms represent toxicity, not tolerance or a desired therapeutic effect.
</details>

---

### Question 149
The nurse is reviewing the morning fasting capillary blood glucose levels for a client diagnosed with Type 2 diabetes. Which of the following values indicates the client is meeting the typical therapeutic goal for fasting blood glucose?

A) 110 mg/dL
B) 55 mg/dL
C) 175 mg/dL
D) 220 mg/dL

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Data Interpretation

**Rationale:** The typical target for a fasting blood glucose level in an adult client with diabetes is between 70 and 130 mg/dL (per American Diabetes Association guidelines). A level of 110 mg/dL falls perfectly within this therapeutic range. A reading of 55 mg/dL indicates hypoglycemia, while 175 mg/dL and 220 mg/dL indicate hyperglycemia.
</details>

---

### Question 150
A client with Type 2 diabetes has successfully recovered from a mild hypoglycemic episode. The client's blood glucose is now stable at 115 mg/dL, and they are ready for breakfast. Which task is appropriate for the nurse to delegate to the Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP)?

A) Evaluate the client's understanding of how to prevent future low blood sugar.
B) Teach the client about the early symptoms of hypoglycemia.
C) Adjust the client's morning insulin dose based on the recent low blood sugar.
D) Provide the client with their scheduled breakfast meal tray.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Delegation

**Rationale:** The UAP can safely perform routine, standard tasks such as delivering meal trays to stable clients. Evaluating understanding (Option A), patient teaching (Option B), and adjusting medication doses (Option C) all require the clinical judgment, assessment, and critical thinking skills of a Registered Nurse (RN) and cannot be legally delegated to a UAP.
</details>
### Question 151
**Lens:** Client Teaching
A nurse is educating a newly diagnosed adult client about the primary difference between Type 1 and Type 2 diabetes mellitus. Which statement by the nurse is the most accurate?

A) "In Type 1 diabetes, your body produces too much insulin, which quickly lowers your blood sugar."
B) "Type 2 diabetes is strictly a childhood illness caused by consuming excessive amounts of sugar."
C) "In Type 1 diabetes, your body does not produce insulin, whereas in Type 2, your body resists the insulin it makes."
D) "Both Type 1 and Type 2 diabetes require you to take oral diabetic pills every single day."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune condition in which the beta cells of the pancreas are destroyed, leading to little or no insulin production. Type 2 diabetes is primarily characterized by insulin resistance (the body does not use insulin properly) and a relative insulin deficiency. Type 1 diabetes requires lifelong insulin therapy, not oral pills.
</details>

---

### Question 152
**Lens:** Procedural Step
A nurse finds a conscious client with Type 1 diabetes who is complaining of shakiness and sweating. The client's capillary blood glucose is 58 mg/dL. What is the nurse's immediate action?

A) Administer 15 grams of a fast-acting oral carbohydrate.
B) Administer a subcutaneous injection of short-acting insulin.
C) Provide a large, hot meal with high protein and fat content.
D) Document the finding and reassess the blood glucose level in one hour.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
For a conscious client experiencing mild to moderate hypoglycemia (blood glucose < 70 mg/dL), the nurse should follow the "Rule of 15." The immediate procedural step is to provide 15 grams of simple, fast-acting carbohydrates (such as 4 oz of fruit juice, regular soda, or glucose tablets) and recheck the blood glucose in 15 minutes. Insulin would further lower the blood sugar, and a high-fat meal slows down the absorption of glucose.
</details>

---

### Question 153
**Lens:** Expected vs Unexpected
A client is taking levothyroxine for the treatment of hypothyroidism. Which of the following findings would indicate to the nurse an unexpected, adverse effect of this medication?

A) Increased daytime energy levels
B) A resting heart rate of 72 beats per minute
C) A mild weight loss of 2 pounds over the last month
D) Palpitations and a resting heart rate of 115 beats per minute

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
Palpitations and tachycardia (a resting heart rate of 115 bpm) are unexpected, adverse effects that indicate a potential overdose of levothyroxine, pushing the client into a state of hyperthyroidism. Increased energy levels, a normal heart rate, and mild weight loss are expected therapeutic outcomes of treating hypothyroidism. 
</details>

---

### Question 154
**Lens:** Risk Recognition
The nurse is reviewing the charts of several clients with diabetes mellitus. Which client is at the highest risk for developing immediate hypoglycemia?

A) A client with Type 2 diabetes who just finished eating a large breakfast
B) A client with Type 1 diabetes who took their morning insulin but refused to eat breakfast
C) A client with Type 2 diabetes who currently has a capillary blood glucose level of 210 mg/dL
D) A client with Type 1 diabetes who forgot to take their scheduled morning dose of insulin

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
Taking insulin without consuming food (carbohydrates) significantly increases the risk of hypoglycemia. The insulin will actively lower blood glucose levels, and without dietary glucose entering the bloodstream to balance it, the client's blood sugar will drop to dangerous levels. Forgetting insulin usually leads to hyperglycemia, not hypoglycemia.
</details>

---

### Question 155
**Lens:** Delegation
A registered nurse (RN) is caring for a stable client with Type 2 diabetes mellitus. Which task is appropriate for the RN to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Administering the client's scheduled oral antidiabetic medication
B) Educating the client on how to properly inspect their feet daily
C) Obtaining the client's capillary blood glucose level before lunch
D) Adjusting the client's sliding-scale insulin dose based on their blood sugar reading

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
Obtaining a capillary blood glucose level (fingerstick) on a stable client is a standard, routine task that safely falls within the scope of practice for a UAP. Medication administration, client education, and clinical decision-making (such as adjusting insulin doses) are strictly nursing responsibilities and cannot be delegated to a UAP.
</details>
### Question 156
A nurse is reviewing the pathophysiology of diabetes mellitus with a newly diagnosed client. Which client statement indicates an expected finding for a diagnosis of Type 1 diabetes rather than Type 2?

A) "This condition mostly develops because of my recent weight gain and lifestyle."
B) "My condition can usually be managed with diet and oral pills alone."
C) "My body no longer produces any insulin, so I will need injections for the rest of my life."
D) "My pancreas produces insulin, but my body's cells are resistant to it."

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune condition characterized by the destruction of pancreatic beta cells, resulting in an absolute deficiency of insulin. Clients with Type 1 diabetes require lifelong exogenous insulin therapy. Options A, B, and D describe the pathophysiology and typical management of Type 2 diabetes, which involves insulin resistance and is highly associated with lifestyle factors.
**Lens:** EXPECTED VS UNEXPECTED
</details>

---

### Question 157
A client with Type 1 diabetes complains of shakiness, sweating, and feeling lightheaded. The nurse checks the client's blood glucose level, which is 58 mg/dL. The client is fully awake and able to swallow. What is the nurse's first procedural step?

A) Administer a subcutaneous injection of emergency glucagon.
B) Provide 15 grams of a fast-acting carbohydrate, such as 4 ounces of fruit juice.
C) Give the client a complex carbohydrate like a turkey sandwich on whole wheat bread.
D) Administer 50% dextrose via rapid intravenous push.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** According to the "Rule of 15" for treating hypoglycemia in a conscious client who can swallow, the first step is to administer 15 grams of a simple, fast-acting carbohydrate (like 4 oz of juice or regular soda) to quickly raise the blood glucose level. Glucagon and IV dextrose are reserved for clients who are unconscious or unable to swallow safely. Complex carbohydrates are given *after* the blood sugar has stabilized to prevent a secondary drop.
**Lens:** PROCEDURAL STEP
</details>

---

### Question 158
The nurse is providing discharge instructions to a client newly prescribed levothyroxine for hypothyroidism. Which statement by the nurse represents correct teaching regarding this medication?

A) "Take this medication at bedtime with a light snack to prevent stomach upset."
B) "You should take this pill in the morning on an empty stomach with a full glass of water."
C) "You can take this pill along with your daily calcium and iron supplements."
D) "Take this medication only on the days when you are feeling sluggish or tired."

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** Levothyroxine must be taken on an empty stomach, typically 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast, with a full glass of water to ensure optimal absorption. Taking it with food or certain supplements (especially calcium and iron) can significantly decrease its absorption. It is a daily maintenance medication, not a PRN (as-needed) drug.
**Lens:** CLIENT TEACHING
</details>

---

### Question 159
A nurse is reviewing the laboratory results of a client with a history of Type 2 diabetes. The client's glycosylated hemoglobin (HbA1c) level is 6.5%. How should the nurse interpret this data?

A) The client's blood sugar has been well-managed over the past three months.
B) The client needs an immediate and drastic increase in their insulin dosage.
C) The client is currently experiencing a severe, acute hypoglycemic episode.
D) The client has extremely poor long-term blood glucose control.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** The HbA1c test measures the average blood glucose level over the past 2 to 3 months. For a client with diabetes, an HbA1c target of less than 7% (and around 6.5% for many clients) indicates good long-term glycemic control. It does not measure acute hypoglycemia, nor does this specific value indicate poor control or a need to increase medication. 
**Lens:** DATA INTERPRETATION
</details>

---

### Question 160
A registered nurse (RN) is caring for a client with Type 2 diabetes who is at risk for hypoglycemia. Which task is appropriate for the RN to delegate to unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Assess the client for signs of confusion, tachycardia, and cold, clammy skin.
B) Evaluate the effectiveness of the carbohydrate snack given to the client 15 minutes ago.
C) Explain the importance of eating scheduled meals to prevent sudden low blood sugar.
D) Perform a bedside capillary blood glucose check before the client eats lunch.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** Performing a routine bedside capillary blood glucose check is a standard, repeatable procedure that falls within the scope of practice for a trained UAP. Assessment (evaluating signs/symptoms), Evaluation (determining if an intervention worked), and Teaching (explaining meal importance) are all nursing responsibilities that require clinical judgment and cannot be delegated.
**Lens:** DELEGATION
</details>
### Question 161
A nurse is educating a newly diagnosed client about the difference between Type 1 and Type 2 diabetes mellitus. Which statement by the nurse provides the most accurate basic explanation?

A: "Type 1 diabetes occurs when your body makes too much insulin, while Type 2 occurs when you make none."
B: "Both types are caused by eating too much sugar, but Type 1 requires insulin injections."
C: "In Type 1 diabetes, your body produces no insulin, while in Type 2, your body is resistant to the insulin it makes."
D: "Type 1 diabetes is only diagnosed in childhood, and Type 2 is only diagnosed in adults over age 50."

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong>
<strong>Lens: Client Teaching</strong>
Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune condition where the beta cells of the pancreas are destroyed, resulting in little to no insulin production. Type 2 diabetes is characterized primarily by insulin resistance, where the body produces insulin but cannot use it effectively, often leading to a progressive decrease in insulin production over time. Option D is incorrect because Type 2 can occur in younger individuals, and Type 1 can be diagnosed in adults. Options A and B contain fundamental inaccuracies regarding the pathophysiology of the disease.
</details>

---

### Question 162
A client with Type 1 diabetes feels shaky and sweaty. Their capillary blood glucose level is 58 mg/dL. The client is awake and able to swallow. What is the nurse's immediate next step?

A: Provide 4 ounces of regular fruit juice.
B: Administer 1 mg of glucagon intramuscularly.
C: Give the client a complex carbohydrate like a turkey sandwich.
D: Administer an extra dose of short-acting insulin.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong>
<strong>Lens: Procedural Step</strong>
For a conscious client with hypoglycemia (blood glucose < 70 mg/dL), the nurse should initiate the "Rule of 15." This involves giving 15 grams of fast-acting carbohydrates, such as 4 ounces (1/2 cup) of regular juice or soda, to quickly raise the blood sugar. Glucagon (Option B) is reserved for clients who are unconscious or unable to swallow safely. A complex carbohydrate (Option C) is given *after* the blood sugar has stabilized to prevent a subsequent drop. Insulin (Option D) would dangerously worsen the hypoglycemia.
</details>

---

### Question 163
A client has been taking levothyroxine for hypothyroidism for the past three weeks. The nurse assesses the client during a follow-up visit. Which clinical finding is an unexpected response to the medication that requires notification of the healthcare provider?

A: The client reports a weight loss of 2 pounds.
B: The client states they have more energy during the day.
C: The client's morning oral temperature is 98.6°F (37°C).
D: The client's resting heart rate is 115 beats per minute.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong>
<strong>Lens: Expected vs Unexpected</strong>
Levothyroxine is a synthetic thyroid hormone used to treat hypothyroidism. A resting heart rate of 115 beats per minute (tachycardia) is an unexpected, adverse effect indicating that the medication dose may be too high, pushing the client into a hyperthyroid state. Expected findings of an effective dose include increased energy (Option B), mild weight loss due to a normalizing metabolism (Option A), and a normal body temperature resolving cold intolerance (Option C). 
</details>

---

### Question 164
A registered nurse (RN) is caring for a client with Type 2 diabetes who has a history of hypoglycemia. Which task is appropriate for the RN to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A: Assess the client for signs of confusion and cold, clammy skin.
B: Perform a capillary fingerstick blood glucose check before meals.
C: Provide education to the client regarding foot care and wearing proper shoes.
D: Determine if the client needs a snack based on their current glucose level.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong>
<strong>Lens: Delegation</strong>
Unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) can perform standard, routine, and safely standardized procedures such as checking capillary blood glucose levels using a glucometer, provided they have been trained and their competency validated. Assessing symptoms (Option A), providing patient education (Option C), and making clinical judgments or decisions based on data (Option D) fall under the nursing scope of practice and cannot be delegated to a UAP.
</details>

---

### Question 165
A client with hypothyroidism has been taking daily levothyroxine for three months. The nurse is reviewing the client's recent laboratory results and symptom diary to evaluate the medication's effectiveness. Which finding indicates the medication is having the desired therapeutic effect?

A: The client's thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH) level has decreased to a normal range.
B: The client reports increasing episodes of constipation and feeling cold.
C: The client's free thyroxine (T4) level is significantly below the normal range.
D: The client states they are sleeping 14 hours a day and still feeling fatigued.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong>
<strong>Lens: Evaluation</strong>
In primary hypothyroidism, the TSH level is typically elevated because the pituitary gland is working hard to stimulate an underactive thyroid. A primary indicator that levothyroxine replacement therapy is effective is the normalization of the TSH level. Options B and D represent classic symptoms of untreated or undertreated hypothyroidism. Option C (low T4) indicates that the circulating thyroid hormone is still insufficient and the medication dose may need to be increased.
</details>
### Question 166
A nurse is reinforcing teaching with a newly diagnosed client about the fundamental differences between Type 1 and Type 2 diabetes mellitus. Which statement by the nurse is accurate?

A. "Type 1 diabetes is usually treated with oral medications, while Type 2 requires lifelong insulin."
B. "Type 1 diabetes only affects older adults, while Type 2 is exclusively seen in young children."
C. "In Type 1 diabetes, your body does not produce insulin, whereas in Type 2, your body resists the insulin it makes."
D. "Both types of diabetes are primarily caused by eating a diet that is too high in concentrated sweets."

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Client Teaching<br>
Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune condition in which the beta cells of the pancreas are destroyed, leading to an absolute lack of insulin production. Type 2 diabetes is primarily characterized by insulin resistance (the body's cells do not respond effectively to insulin) and a relative insulin deficiency. Option A is backward. Option B is incorrect as Type 1 is often diagnosed in youth, and Type 2 is increasingly diagnosed at all ages. Option D is a common myth; diet alone does not directly cause Type 1 diabetes.
</details>

---

### Question 167
A nurse is observing a client who has a history of diabetes mellitus. Which of the following findings is an expected early sign of hypoglycemia?

A. Diaphoresis and trembling
B. Deep, rapid breathing and fruity breath
C. Bradycardia and flushed, warm skin
D. Increased urination and extreme thirst

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Expected VS Unexpected<br>
Early expected signs of hypoglycemia include diaphoresis (sweating), trembling, tachycardia, and irritability. These symptoms are primarily caused by the release of epinephrine as the body attempts to raise blood glucose levels. Deep, rapid breathing and fruity breath (Option B) are signs of diabetic ketoacidosis. Flushed skin and bradycardia (Option C) do not correlate with hypoglycemia. Increased urination and extreme thirst (Option D) are classic signs of hyperglycemia.
</details>

---

### Question 168
A nurse is instructing a client on the proper procedure for taking their newly prescribed levothyroxine for hypothyroidism. Which procedural instruction should the nurse provide?

A. "Take the medication with a high-calcium meal to boost absorption."
B. "Take the medication right before going to bed with a small carbohydrate snack."
C. "Take the medication as needed whenever you feel excessively fatigued or sluggish."
D. "Take the medication in the morning on an empty stomach with a full glass of water."

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Procedural Step<br>
Levothyroxine should be administered first thing in the morning on an empty stomach, at least 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast, with a full glass of water. This ensures optimal and consistent absorption of the medication. Taking it with food or supplements (like calcium or iron) can significantly decrease its absorption. It must be taken daily, not on an "as needed" basis. 
</details>

---

### Question 169
An RN is working with an Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP) on a medical-surgical unit. A client with diabetes reports feeling shaky and hungry. Which task is appropriate for the RN to delegate to the UAP?

A. Determine if the client needs half a cup of fruit juice or a glucagon injection.
B. Perform a capillary blood glucose check on the client.
C. Teach the client about the signs and symptoms of low blood sugar.
D. Assess the client's current level of consciousness and neurologic status.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Delegation<br>
Performing a capillary blood glucose check is a standard, routine procedure that is within the scope of practice for a UAP. The RN must assess the client's neurologic status (Option D), determine the appropriate clinical intervention (Option A), and provide client education (Option C), as these tasks require nursing judgment and cannot be delegated.
</details>

---

### Question 170
A client has been taking levothyroxine for the treatment of hypothyroidism for the past three months. Which client statement indicates to the nurse that the medication is having its desired therapeutic effect?

A. "I have more energy during the day and am no longer feeling constantly cold."
B. "I have been sleeping 12 hours a day and still feel absolutely exhausted."
C. "My heart feels like it is fluttering constantly and I am losing weight rapidly."
D. "My bowel movements have stopped almost completely and I am very constipated."

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Evaluation<br>
Hypothyroidism causes a slowed metabolism, leading to symptoms like fatigue, cold intolerance, weight gain, and constipation. An improvement in these symptoms, such as increased energy and normal temperature regulation, indicates that the levothyroxine dose is effective. Options B and D indicate the medication is not yet effective (ongoing hypothyroidism). Option C describes symptoms of hyperthyroidism, indicating the dosage may be too high (toxicity).
</details>
### Question 171
A nurse is educating a client who is newly prescribed levothyroxine for hypothyroidism. Which statement by the client indicates an understanding of the teaching?

A) "I should take this medication with my breakfast."
B) "I will take this pill on an empty stomach in the morning."
C) "I can take this medication right before I go to sleep."
D) "I need to take this pill with an antacid to prevent stomach upset."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Client Teaching
**System Focus:** Thyroid Medication

Levothyroxine should be taken on an empty stomach, usually 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast, to ensure maximum and consistent absorption. Taking it with food, calcium supplements, or antacids can significantly decrease its absorption.

</details>

---

### Question 172
A nurse is reviewing the laboratory results of a client being screened for type 2 diabetes mellitus. Which fasting blood glucose level indicates a normal, healthy finding?

A) 145 mg/dL
B) 115 mg/dL
C) 85 mg/dL
D) 130 mg/dL

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Data Interpretation
**System Focus:** Type 1 vs Type 2 Basics

A normal fasting blood glucose level is typically between 70 and 99 mg/dL. Values between 100 and 125 mg/dL indicate prediabetes, and a fasting blood glucose of 126 mg/dL or higher on two separate occasions is diagnostic for diabetes.

</details>

---

### Question 173
An awake and alert client with type 1 diabetes reports feeling shaky and sweating profusely. The nurse checks the client's blood glucose, which is 58 mg/dL. What is the nurse's immediate next step?

A) Administer 15 grams of a fast-acting oral carbohydrate.
B) Administer 1 milligram of intramuscular glucagon.
C) Administer a rapid-acting insulin bolus.
D) Recheck the blood glucose level in 30 minutes.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Procedural Step
**System Focus:** Hypoglycemia

The "Rule of 15" for a conscious and alert client experiencing hypoglycemia involves giving 15 grams of fast-acting carbohydrates (such as 4 ounces of fruit juice or regular soda) and rechecking the blood glucose in 15 minutes. Intramuscular glucagon is reserved for clients who are unconscious or unable to swallow safely. 

</details>

---

### Question 174
A pediatric nurse is assessing a child recently diagnosed with type 1 diabetes mellitus. Which of the following sets of symptoms is expected prior to the initiation of insulin therapy?

A) Weight gain, bradycardia, and cold intolerance.
B) Oliguria, edema, and hypertension.
C) Decreased appetite, constipation, and dry skin.
D) Increased thirst, frequent urination, and excessive hunger.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Expected vs Unexpected
**System Focus:** Type 1 vs Type 2 Basics

The classic "3 Ps" of type 1 diabetes mellitus are polydipsia (increased thirst), polyuria (frequent urination), and polyphagia (excessive hunger). These symptoms occur as a direct result of untreated hyperglycemia and the body's inability to use glucose for energy without insulin.

</details>

---

### Question 175
A registered nurse (RN) is caring for a client with type 2 diabetes who has a scheduled capillary blood glucose check before lunch. Which task can the RN safely delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Assessing the client for signs of hypoglycemia.
B) Performing the capillary blood glucose measurement.
C) Educating the client on how to use the glucometer at home.
D) Determining the correct dose of sliding-scale insulin to administer.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Delegation
**System Focus:** Hypoglycemia & Diabetes Basics

Unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) are trained to perform standard, routine tasks such as capillary blood glucose measurements (fingersticks). However, assessing the client, providing client education, and determining medication dosages fall strictly within the scope of practice of the registered nurse and cannot be delegated.

</details>
### Question 176
**Lens:** Client Teaching
**Topic:** Thyroid Medication

A nurse is providing discharge instructions to a client newly prescribed levothyroxine for hypothyroidism. Which statement by the client indicates an understanding of the teaching?

A) "I will take this medication with my breakfast."
B) "I should take this pill every morning on an empty stomach."
C) "I can stop taking the medication when my energy returns."
D) "I will take it before bedtime with a glass of milk."

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) "I should take this pill every morning on an empty stomach."

**Rationale:** Levothyroxine is a thyroid hormone replacement medication that must be taken in the morning on an empty stomach, at least 30 to 60 minutes before the first meal of the day. Taking it with food or certain beverages (like milk) significantly decreases its absorption. The medication is a lifelong therapy and should not be stopped once symptoms improve.
</details>

---

### Question 177
**Lens:** Expected vs Unexpected
**Topic:** Type 1 vs Type 2 Basics

The nurse is assessing a young adult client who is being evaluated for new-onset type 1 diabetes mellitus. Which of the following is an expected clinical manifestation specific to the initial onset of this disease?

A) Unexplained weight gain
B) Decreased urine output
C) Unexplained weight loss
D) Lack of thirst

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Unexplained weight loss

**Rationale:** Type 1 diabetes is characterized by an absolute lack of insulin. Without insulin, glucose cannot enter the cells to be used for energy. As a result, the body begins to break down fat and muscle tissue for energy, leading to expected, unexplained weight loss despite normal or increased food intake (polyphagia). Increased urine output (polyuria) and increased thirst (polydipsia) are also expected findings.
</details>

---

### Question 178
**Lens:** Procedural Step
**Topic:** Hypoglycemia

A client with type 2 diabetes is awake, alert, and reports feeling shaky and sweating. The nurse checks the client's blood glucose level, which is 58 mg/dL (3.2 mmol/L). What is the nurse's first procedural step?

A) Administer 15 grams of a fast-acting oral carbohydrate.
B) Give a subcutaneous injection of glucagon.
C) Administer 50% dextrose via intravenous push.
D) Provide a high-protein snack like cheese and crackers.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) Administer 15 grams of a fast-acting oral carbohydrate.

**Rationale:** For a conscious and alert client experiencing mild hypoglycemia, the standard procedural step is to follow the "Rule of 15" by providing 15 grams of a fast-acting oral carbohydrate (e.g., 4 oz of fruit juice or regular soda). Subcutaneous glucagon and IV dextrose are reserved for clients who are unconscious, seizing, or unable to safely swallow. A high-protein snack is given *after* the blood glucose has stabilized to prevent subsequent drops.
</details>

---

### Question 179
**Lens:** Delegation
**Topic:** Diabetes Basics

The registered nurse (RN) is caring for a client with type 2 diabetes. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Assessing the client's feet for signs of neuropathy.
B) Teaching the client how to use a new blood glucose meter.
C) Determining the insulin dose based on the sliding scale.
D) Performing a capillary fingerstick blood glucose test before lunch.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Performing a capillary fingerstick blood glucose test before lunch.

**Rationale:** Routine capillary blood glucose monitoring is a standard, repeatable procedural task that can be safely delegated to trained UAP. Assessing, teaching, and calculating medication doses require nursing judgment and fall strictly within the RN's scope of practice; these tasks cannot be delegated to UAP.
</details>

---

### Question 180
**Lens:** Data Interpretation
**Topic:** Diabetes Basics

The nurse is reviewing the laboratory results for a client with a history of type 2 diabetes mellitus. The client's hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c) level is reported as 6.5%. How should the nurse interpret this data?

A) The client requires an immediate increase in their insulin regimen.
B) The client has demonstrated good long-term glycemic control.
C) The client is currently experiencing an episode of hypoglycemia.
D) The client is experiencing severe, life-threatening hyperglycemia.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) The client has demonstrated good long-term glycemic control.

**Rationale:** The hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c) test measures the average blood glucose control over the past 2 to 3 months. For a client diagnosed with diabetes, the general target is an HbA1c level of less than 7%. Therefore, a result of 6.5% indicates good, expected glycemic control. It does not reflect an acute hypoglycemic or life-threatening hyperglycemic emergency.
</details>
### Question 181
**Lens:** Client Teaching
The nurse is teaching a client newly diagnosed with Type 1 diabetes mellitus. The client asks, "Why do I need insulin shots when my friend with Type 2 diabetes just takes a pill?" Which is the best response by the nurse?

A) "Your body produces too much insulin, so the shots help regulate the excess."
B) "In Type 1 diabetes, your pancreas no longer makes insulin, so it must be replaced."
C) "Pills are only used for Type 2 diabetes because their blood sugar levels are much higher."
D) "You will only need insulin shots until your pancreas heals and starts working again."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune condition in which the beta cells of the pancreas are destroyed, resulting in an absolute lack of insulin production. Because the body makes no insulin on its own, lifelong exogenous insulin therapy (via injections or a pump) is required. Type 2 diabetes involves insulin resistance and decreased insulin production, which can often be managed with oral medications (pills) that improve insulin sensitivity or stimulate the remaining beta cells.
</details>

---

### Question 182
**Lens:** Procedural Step
A conscious and alert client with Type 2 diabetes reports feeling shaky, weak, and dizzy. The nurse checks the capillary blood glucose level, which is 58 mg/dL. What is the nurse's next procedural step?

A) Administer 1 mg of glucagon intramuscularly.
B) Provide a high-protein snack, such as a slice of cheese.
C) Give the client 4 ounces of apple juice to drink.
D) Recheck the blood glucose level in 15 minutes.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
The procedural step for treating a conscious client with acute hypoglycemia (blood glucose < 70 mg/dL) follows the "Rule of 15." The nurse should immediately administer 15 grams of fast-acting, simple carbohydrates, such as 4 ounces of fruit juice or regular soda. Rechecking the blood glucose (Option D) is done 15 minutes *after* administering the carbohydrate. Glucagon (Option A) is reserved for unconscious clients or those unable to swallow. Protein (Option B) is given after the blood sugar is stabilized, not as the initial rescue treatment.
</details>

---

### Question 183
**Lens:** Evaluation
A client with hypothyroidism has been taking daily levothyroxine for the past two months. Which statement by the client indicates to the nurse that the medication therapy is effective?

A) "I have noticed I have more energy and I am not feeling cold all the time."
B) "My heart feels like it is racing and I cannot seem to sit still."
C) "I have gained about five pounds over the last month."
D) "I am sleeping almost 12 hours a day and still feel exhausted."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
Levothyroxine is a synthetic thyroid hormone used to treat hypothyroidism. The evaluation of its effectiveness is based on the resolution of classic hypothyroid symptoms, such as fatigue, cold intolerance, and weight gain. Option A indicates a therapeutic response. Option B describes symptoms of hyperthyroidism, suggesting the medication dose may be too high. Options C and D indicate that the hypothyroid symptoms are still present and the medication is not yet fully effective.
</details>

---

### Question 184
**Lens:** Data Interpretation
The nurse is reviewing the laboratory results of a client diagnosed with Type 2 diabetes. Which laboratory value indicates long-term adherence and good glycemic control?

A) A fasting blood glucose level of 180 mg/dL.
B) A postprandial blood glucose level of 250 mg/dL.
C) A Hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c) level of 9.2%.
D) A Hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c) level of 6.8%.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
Hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c) measures the percentage of hemoglobin carrying sugar and reflects the average blood glucose level over the previous 2 to 3 months. For most non-pregnant adults with diabetes, an HbA1c goal of less than 7% indicates good long-term glycemic control. A value of 9.2% (Option C), a fasting glucose of 180 mg/dL (Option A), and a postprandial glucose of 250 mg/dL (Option B) all indicate poor blood glucose management.
</details>

---

### Question 185
**Lens:** Expected vs Unexpected
The nurse administers a morning dose of regular insulin to a client. Two hours later, the nurse observes the client's condition. Which finding is an unexpected complication that requires immediate intervention?

A) The client is resting quietly in bed while reading a book.
B) The client is sweating profusely, has mild tremors, and appears confused.
C) The client states they are feeling hungry and requests their lunch tray.
D) The client's capillary blood glucose reading is 110 mg/dL.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
Regular insulin is a short-acting insulin that peaks in about 2 to 5 hours, placing the client at high risk for hypoglycemia during this timeframe. Sweating (diaphoresis), tremors, and confusion are unexpected, acute signs of hypoglycemia that require immediate nursing intervention (carbohydrate administration). Being hungry, resting quietly, and having a normal blood glucose reading of 110 mg/dL are all expected or non-urgent findings.
</details>
### Question 186
A nurse is educating a client who has been newly prescribed levothyroxine for hypothyroidism. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of how to take this medication?

A) "I should take my pill every evening right before I go to bed with a glass of milk."
B) "I can take this medication with an antacid if it starts to upset my stomach."
C) "I need to take this medication in the morning on an empty stomach."
D) "I will take the pill right after I finish eating my breakfast to help with absorption."

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: C</b><br>
<b>Lens:</b> Client Teaching<br>
Levothyroxine should be taken in the morning on an empty stomach, at least 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast. This ensures optimal absorption of the medication and helps prevent insomnia that could occur if taken later in the day. Antacids, calcium, iron, and food can interfere with the absorption of thyroid replacement hormones (making B and D incorrect). Taking it at bedtime is not recommended due to the potential for sleep disturbances (making A incorrect).
</details>

---

### Question 187
A nurse is reviewing the pathophysiology of diabetes mellitus with a nursing student. Which characteristic is an expected finding associated primarily with Type 1 diabetes, rather than Type 2 diabetes?

A) A gradual onset of symptoms occurring over several years
B) An absolute lack of endogenous insulin production
C) A strong association with obesity and a sedentary lifestyle
D) Initial treatment usually consists of oral hypoglycemic agents

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: B</b><br>
<b>Lens:</b> Expected vs Unexpected<br>
Type 1 diabetes is characterized by the autoimmune destruction of the pancreatic beta cells, resulting in an absolute lack of endogenous insulin production. Therefore, clients with Type 1 diabetes are dependent on exogenous insulin for survival. Options A, C, and D are expected characteristics of Type 2 diabetes, which is characterized by insulin resistance, often develops gradually, is strongly linked to lifestyle factors, and can frequently be treated initially with oral medications.
</details>

---

### Question 188
A client with diabetes reports feeling shaky, sweaty, and lightheaded. The nurse checks the client's blood glucose level, which reads 58 mg/dL. The client is fully awake and able to swallow safely. What is the nurse's immediate next step?

A) Administer 4 ounces of regular, non-diet fruit juice.
B) Administer 1 mg of glucagon intramuscularly.
C) Provide a complex carbohydrate and a protein snack.
D) Wait 15 minutes and recheck the capillary blood glucose level.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: A</b><br>
<b>Lens:</b> Procedural Step<br>
The client is experiencing hypoglycemia (blood glucose < 70 mg/dL). For a conscious client who can swallow safely, the nurse should immediately follow the "Rule of 15" by providing 15 grams of simple, fast-acting carbohydrates, such as 4 ounces of regular fruit juice or regular soda. Glucagon (Option B) is reserved for clients who are unconscious or unable to swallow safely. A complex carbohydrate and protein snack (Option C) is given *after* the hypoglycemia is corrected with simple sugars to stabilize the blood glucose. Waiting 15 minutes without treating (Option D) will cause the client's condition to worsen.
</details>

---

### Question 189
A registered nurse (RN) is managing the care of a client admitted with poorly controlled Type 2 diabetes mellitus. Which task is appropriate for the RN to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Assessing the client's lower extremities for signs of diminished sensation.
B) Educating the client on the common signs and symptoms of hypoglycemia.
C) Administering a scheduled dose of subcutaneous regular insulin before lunch.
D) Obtaining a routine capillary blood glucose measurement before meals.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: D</b><br>
<b>Lens:</b> Delegation<br>
Obtaining a routine capillary blood glucose measurement is a standard procedure that falls safely within the scope of practice for a trained UAP. Assessment (Option A), patient education (Option B), and medication administration (Option C) require nursing judgment and a nursing license, and therefore cannot be delegated to a UAP.
</details>

---

### Question 190
A young adult client newly diagnosed with Type 1 diabetes says to the nurse, "I don't understand why I have to take shots. My grandfather has diabetes and he just takes a pill." Which response by the nurse is the most therapeutic and accurate?

A) "Your grandfather has Type 2 diabetes, which is a completely different disease than what you have."
B) "Your body has stopped making insulin, so you need the shots to replace it. It is completely normal to feel overwhelmed by this."
C) "Don't worry too much. Eventually, you might be able to switch to pills if you manage your diet well."
D) "You will need to ask your healthcare provider why they chose this specific treatment plan for you."

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: B</b><br>
<b>Lens:</b> Therapeutic Communication<br>
This response directly and simply explains the pathophysiology of Type 1 diabetes (lack of insulin production requiring injection) while actively validating the client's feelings of being overwhelmed. Option A is somewhat dismissive and lacks a clear educational explanation. Option C provides false reassurance and incorrect information, as Type 1 diabetes always requires insulin replacement and cannot be managed with oral pills alone. Option D blocks communication and inappropriately passes a basic nursing education responsibility to the provider.
</details>
### Question 191
A nurse is providing education to a client newly diagnosed with Type 1 diabetes mellitus. Which statement by the nurse accurately describes the underlying cause of this specific condition?

A. "Your body still produces insulin, but your cells have become highly resistant to it."
B. "This condition is primarily managed with strict diet control and oral antidiabetic pills."
C. "Your pancreas no longer produces insulin, requiring you to take insulin therapy for life."
D. "Your liver is producing too much glucose, which causes your blood sugar to stay high."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Client Teaching
**System Focus:** Type 1 vs Type 2 basics

**Rationale:** Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune condition where the beta cells of the pancreas are destroyed, resulting in an absolute lack of insulin. Therefore, clients with Type 1 diabetes require exogenous insulin therapy for life to survive. Option A describes the pathophysiology of Type 2 diabetes (insulin resistance). Option B describes the typical initial management of Type 2 diabetes. Option D describes hepatic glucose overproduction, which is a contributing factor in Type 2 diabetes, not the primary cause of Type 1.
</details>

---

### Question 192
A nurse finds an alert and oriented client with a blood glucose level of 58 mg/dL. The client reports feeling shaky, sweaty, and hungry. According to the 15-15 rule for managing hypoglycemia, what is the nurse's immediate next step?

A. Provide 4 ounces of regular, non-diet fruit juice.
B. Administer 1 mg of glucagon intramuscularly.
C. Give the client a high-protein snack like a handful of almonds.
D. Administer 50% dextrose via an intravenous push.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Procedural Step
**System Focus:** Hypoglycemia

**Rationale:** For an alert, conscious client experiencing mild hypoglycemia, the nurse should follow the 15-15 rule: administer 15 grams of simple, fast-acting carbohydrates (such as 4 ounces of regular fruit juice or soda) and recheck the blood glucose in 15 minutes. Glucagon (Option B) and IV Dextrose (Option D) are reserved for clients who are unconscious or unable to swallow safely. High-protein or high-fat snacks (Option C) delay the absorption of glucose and should not be used as the initial treatment for an acute hypoglycemic episode.
</details>

---

### Question 193
A client with hypothyroidism has been taking levothyroxine daily for the past three months. During a follow-up visit, which finding indicates to the nurse that the medication is having the expected therapeutic effect?

A. The client reports a recent, unintended decrease in their resting heart rate.
B. The client's skin feels abnormally cool and clammy to the touch.
C. The client states they have gained 5 pounds over the last month.
D. The client reports having increased energy levels and less daily fatigue.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Expected vs Unexpected
**System Focus:** Thyroid medication

**Rationale:** Levothyroxine is a synthetic thyroid hormone used to treat hypothyroidism. Expected therapeutic effects include a reversal of hypothyroid symptoms, such as an increase in energy, decreased fatigue, weight loss, and an improved tolerance to cold. Fatigue is a hallmark symptom of hypothyroidism. Bradycardia (Option A), cool skin (Option B), and weight gain (Option C) are all signs of continuing or worsening hypothyroidism, indicating the medication is not yet effective or the dose is too low.
</details>

---

### Question 194
The nurse is reviewing the routine laboratory results for a client diagnosed with Type 2 diabetes mellitus. The client's hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c) level is 6.5%. How should the nurse interpret this data?

A. The client's blood sugar was significantly elevated specifically over the last 24 hours.
B. The client's blood glucose has been well-controlled over the past 2 to 3 months.
C. The client needs an immediate increase in their prescribed oral antidiabetic medication.
D. The client has been experiencing severe, recurring episodes of hypoglycemia.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Data Interpretation
**System Focus:** Type 1 vs Type 2 basics

**Rationale:** The hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c) test measures the average blood glucose control over the lifespan of a red blood cell, which is approximately 2 to 3 months. For most non-pregnant adults with diabetes, the American Diabetes Association recommends an HbA1c goal of less than 7.0%. A level of 6.5% indicates good, long-term glycemic control. It does not reflect a specific 24-hour period (Option A), does not require an immediate medication increase (Option C), and does not indicate hypoglycemia (Option D).
</details>

---

### Question 195
A client newly diagnosed with Type 2 diabetes tells the nurse, "I am so scared I will have to give myself shots for the rest of my life like my friend with Type 1 diabetes." What is the most therapeutic response by the nurse?

A. "Why are you so afraid of taking daily insulin injections?"
B. "Don't worry, Type 2 diabetes is never treated with insulin shots."
C. "Type 2 diabetes is often managed first with lifestyle changes and pills, but let's talk about your specific treatment plan."
D. "You will only need shots if you fail to follow your recommended diet and exercise program."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Therapeutic Communication
**System Focus:** Type 1 vs Type 2 basics

**Rationale:** Option C addresses the client's fear while providing accurate, factual information about the standard initial treatment for Type 2 diabetes (lifestyle modifications and oral agents) and opens the door for further discussion. Option A uses "why," which can make the client feel defensive and is non-therapeutic. Option B provides false reassurance, as clients with Type 2 diabetes may eventually require insulin. Option D is punitive and places blame on the client, which damages the therapeutic nurse-client relationship.
</details>
### Question 196
A nurse is reinforcing teaching with a client who has a new prescription for levothyroxine for the treatment of hypothyroidism. Which instruction should the nurse include?

A) "Take this medication with meals to prevent stomach upset."
B) "Take this medication in the morning on an empty stomach."
C) "Take this medication right before going to bed."
D) "Take this medication only when you feel fatigued."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: B**

**Lens:** Client Teaching
**System Focus:** Thyroid Medication

Levothyroxine is a synthetic thyroid hormone used to treat hypothyroidism. It should be taken on an empty stomach, typically 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast, to ensure optimal and consistent absorption. Taking it with food or at night can interfere with its absorption and effectiveness. It is a daily maintenance medication, not taken as needed for fatigue. 

</details>

---

### Question 197
A nurse is monitoring a client who has type 1 diabetes mellitus. Which of the following findings should the nurse identify as an expected clinical manifestation of hypoglycemia?

A) Increased urination and extreme thirst
B) Deep, rapid breathing and fruity breath
C) Diaphoresis and shakiness
D) Flushed, warm, and dry skin

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: C**

**Lens:** Expected vs Unexpected
**System Focus:** Hypoglycemia

Diaphoresis (sweating), shakiness, tremors, tachycardia, and confusion are all expected classic signs of hypoglycemia (low blood sugar). Options A, B, and D are classic signs of hyperglycemia (high blood sugar) and diabetic ketoacidosis.

</details>

---

### Question 198
A nurse is reviewing educational materials for a community health class. Which of the following statements accurately interprets the primary physiological difference between type 1 and type 2 diabetes mellitus?

A) Type 1 diabetes only affects older adults, while type 2 diabetes occurs exclusively in childhood.
B) Type 1 diabetes results from eating too much sugar, while type 2 is strictly genetic.
C) Type 1 diabetes is managed solely with diet, while type 2 diabetes always requires insulin injections.
D) Type 1 diabetes is characterized by an absolute lack of insulin production, while type 2 involves insulin resistance.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: D**

**Lens:** Data Interpretation
**System Focus:** Type 1 vs Type 2 Basics

The fundamental difference between the two types of diabetes is their underlying pathophysiology. Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune condition resulting in the destruction of pancreatic beta cells, leading to an absolute lack of insulin. Type 2 diabetes is primarily characterized by cellular insulin resistance and a relative, rather than absolute, insulin deficiency.

</details>

---

### Question 199
A conscious client with type 1 diabetes reports feeling dizzy, weak, and hungry. Their capillary blood glucose level is verified at 55 mg/dL. What is the correct procedural step the nurse should take to treat this client?

A) Administer 15 grams of a fast-acting carbohydrate.
B) Provide a glass of diet cola with ice.
C) Administer 10 units of regular insulin subcutaneously.
D) Give the client a high-protein, high-fat meal.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: A**

**Lens:** Procedural Step
**System Focus:** Hypoglycemia

According to the "Rule of 15" for a conscious hypoglycemic patient, the nurse should immediately administer 15 grams of a fast-acting carbohydrate (e.g., 4 oz of regular juice, glucose tablets). Diet cola contains no sugar and will not raise blood glucose. Insulin will lower blood glucose further, worsening the condition. High-fat/high-protein foods will delay the absorption of glucose.

</details>

---

### Question 200
A nurse is evaluating the understanding of a client who is newly diagnosed with type 2 diabetes mellitus. Which client statement indicates that the teaching was successful?

A) "I will need to take insulin injections for the rest of my life."
B) "My pancreas has completely stopped making insulin."
C) "Losing weight and exercising can help my body use its own insulin better."
D) "I am immune to developing hypoglycemia since I have type 2 diabetes."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: C**

**Lens:** Evaluation
**System Focus:** Type 1 vs Type 2 Basics

In type 2 diabetes, the body still produces insulin but the cells are resistant to it. Lifestyle modifications, such as weight loss and regular exercise, can decrease insulin resistance and improve glucose control. Clients with type 2 diabetes do not necessarily need lifelong insulin (many are managed with oral medications or diet), their pancreas still produces some insulin, and they can still experience hypoglycemia, especially if taking certain oral anti-diabetic medications or insulin.

</details>
### Question 201
A nurse is reviewing the capillary blood glucose levels for a client with diabetes who is feeling dizzy and weak. Which of the following blood glucose values indicates hypoglycemia and requires immediate treatment?

A) 110 mg/dL
B) 85 mg/dL
C) 60 mg/dL
D) 140 mg/dL

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C <br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Data Interpretation <br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Hypoglycemia is clinically defined as a blood glucose level below 70 mg/dL. A blood glucose of 60 mg/dL is low and correlates with the client's symptoms of dizziness and weakness, requiring immediate intervention. Levels of 85 mg/dL and 110 mg/dL are within normal fasting or pre-meal ranges, and 140 mg/dL is slightly elevated or a normal post-meal level.
</details>

### Question 202
A nurse is providing teaching to a client newly diagnosed with type 1 diabetes mellitus. Which statement by the nurse accurately describes the primary cause of this specific condition?

A) "Your body has become resistant to the insulin it produces, usually due to lifestyle factors."
B) "Your pancreas no longer produces insulin because the insulin-producing cells have been destroyed."
C) "Your liver is releasing too much glucose into your bloodstream when you are fasting."
D) "Your kidneys are unable to filter the excess sugar out of your urine effectively."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> B <br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Client Teaching <br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune condition characterized by an absolute lack of insulin production due to the destruction of pancreatic beta cells. Option A describes type 2 diabetes (insulin resistance). Options C and D do not describe the primary etiology of type 1 diabetes.
</details>

---

### Question 203
A client is taking daily levothyroxine for the treatment of hypothyroidism. During a follow-up visit, the nurse assesses the client. Which finding is an unexpected sign that the medication dose may be too high?

A) Resting heart rate of 115 beats per minute
B) Decreased feelings of daily fatigue
C) A soft, formed bowel movement every morning
D) Weight loss of 2 pounds over the last month

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A <br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Expected vs Unexpected <br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Tachycardia (a resting heart rate greater than 100 beats per minute) is an unexpected finding that indicates potential hyperthyroidism, which is a sign of levothyroxine toxicity (too high of a dose). Decreased fatigue, regular bowel movements, and slight weight loss are expected, desired therapeutic outcomes of correcting hypothyroidism.
</details>

---

### Question 204
A nurse finds a conscious, alert client with diabetes exhibiting tremors and diaphoresis. The client's blood glucose level is 58 mg/dL. According to the "Rule of 15" for hypoglycemia, what is the nurse's immediate procedural step?

A) Administer 1 mg of glucagon intramuscularly.
B) Provide a complex carbohydrate and a high-protein snack.
C) Recheck the blood glucose level on a different finger to confirm.
D) Give 15 grams of a fast-acting, simple carbohydrate.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> D <br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Procedural Step <br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> The standard procedural step for treating hypoglycemia in a conscious, alert client is the "Rule of 15": give 15 grams of a fast-acting simple carbohydrate (such as 4 ounces of fruit juice or regular soda), wait 15 minutes, and recheck the blood sugar. Glucagon is reserved for unconscious clients or those unable to swallow. A complex carbohydrate and protein are given *after* the blood sugar has stabilized. 
</details>

---

### Question 205
A nurse evaluates the effectiveness of levothyroxine therapy for a client with a history of severe hypothyroidism. Which client statement best indicates that the medication is having its intended therapeutic effect?

A) "I am finally able to sleep 12 hours a day without waking up."
B) "I have noticed my hair is falling out more than usual."
C) "I don't need to wear a heavy sweater inside the house anymore."
D) "My heart feels like it is constantly racing."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C <br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Evaluation <br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Cold intolerance is a classic symptom of hypothyroidism due to a low basal metabolic rate. Stating that they no longer need a heavy sweater indoors indicates that their metabolic rate is increasing to a normal level, demonstrating the medication is effective. Sleeping excessively and hair loss are signs of ongoing hypothyroidism. A racing heart is an adverse effect indicating the dose may be too high.
</details>
### Question 206
A nurse is educating a client newly diagnosed with Type 1 diabetes mellitus about their condition. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the teaching?

A) "My body still makes insulin, but my cells just do not respond well to it."
B) "I can manage my blood sugar purely by eating a healthy diet and exercising."
C) "My pancreas no longer produces insulin, so I will need insulin injections."
D) "This condition developed because I ate too much sugar when I was younger."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C<br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Client Teaching<br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune condition in which the beta cells of the pancreas are destroyed, leading to an absolute lack of insulin production. Therefore, clients with Type 1 diabetes require lifelong exogenous insulin therapy. Option A describes the pathophysiology of Type 2 diabetes (insulin resistance). Option B is incorrect because diet and exercise alone cannot manage Type 1 diabetes, though they are important for overall health. Option D is a common myth; eating sugar does not cause Type 1 diabetes.
</details>

---

### Question 207
A nurse is collecting data on a client who received a dose of regular insulin a few hours ago. Which of the following findings is an expected physiological manifestation of mild hypoglycemia?

A) Diaphoresis and shakiness
B) Deep, rapid breathing
C) Flushed, dry skin
D) Extreme thirst and frequent urination

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A<br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Expected vs Unexpected<br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Hypoglycemia (low blood sugar) triggers the release of epinephrine, which causes sympathetic nervous system symptoms such as diaphoresis (sweating), shakiness, tachycardia, and anxiety. These are expected expected early warning signs of low blood sugar. Options B, C, and D are classic signs of hyperglycemia (high blood sugar) and diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA), which include Kussmaul respirations, dehydration, polydipsia, and polyuria.
</details>

---

### Question 208
A nurse is providing instructions to a client who has been prescribed levothyroxine for hypothyroidism. Which procedural step should the nurse include for the proper administration of this medication?

A) Take the medication right after eating a large breakfast.
B) Take the medication at bedtime with a glass of milk.
C) Take the medication alongside an antacid to prevent stomach upset.
D) Take the medication in the morning on an empty stomach.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> D<br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Procedural Step<br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Levothyroxine absorption is highly influenced by food and other medications. The correct procedural step is to take the medication first thing in the morning on an empty stomach, at least 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast. Option A is incorrect because food decreases absorption. Options B and C are incorrect because calcium (milk, antacids) and iron supplements severely impair the absorption of levothyroxine.
</details>

---

### Question 209
A nurse is caring for a client who recently experienced a mild hypoglycemic episode but is now conscious, alert, and has a stable blood glucose level of 95 mg/dL. Which task is appropriate to delegate to the Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP)?

A) Assess the client's understanding of what triggered the low blood sugar.
B) Provide the client with a scheduled complex carbohydrate and protein snack.
C) Adjust the client's next scheduled insulin dose based on the recent event.
D) Evaluate the client for subtle neurological signs of recurrent hypoglycemia.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> B<br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Delegation<br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Providing food or a scheduled snack to a stable client is a routine task that falls safely within the UAP's scope of practice. Assessing a client's knowledge (Option A), adjusting medication doses (Option C), and evaluating a client's clinical status (Option D) all require nursing judgment and are strictly the responsibility of the registered nurse or licensed practical nurse.
</details>

---

### Question 210
A nurse is reviewing health histories for participants at a community health screening. Which factor places a client at the highest risk for developing Type 2 diabetes mellitus?

A) Being overweight and maintaining a sedentary lifestyle
B) A recent viral infection affecting the pancreas
C) Having a primary medical history of an autoimmune disease
D) Consuming a diet primarily consisting of lean proteins and vegetables

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A<br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Risk Recognition<br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> The most significant, modifiable risk factors for developing Type 2 diabetes are obesity (being overweight) and physical inactivity (a sedentary lifestyle), as these contribute directly to insulin resistance. Options B and C are risk factors more closely associated with the development of Type 1 diabetes, which involves autoimmune destruction of pancreatic beta cells often triggered by an environmental factor like a virus. Option D represents a healthy dietary pattern that actually reduces the risk of developing Type 2 diabetes.
</details>
### Question 211
A nurse is educating a client who has just been diagnosed with Type 1 diabetes mellitus. Which statement by the nurse accurately describes the primary cause of this condition?

A) "Your body has developed a resistance to the insulin your pancreas produces over time."
B) "Your pancreas no longer produces insulin due to the autoimmune destruction of its beta cells."
C) "Consuming too many sugary foods over the years has permanently worn out your pancreas."
D) "Your liver is releasing too much stored glucose into your bloodstream, causing high sugar levels."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Client Teaching
**System Focus:** Type 1 vs Type 2 basics

**Rationale:** Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune disorder where the body's immune system mistakenly attacks and destroys the insulin-producing beta cells in the pancreas, leading to an absolute lack of insulin. Option A describes Type 2 diabetes mellitus, which is characterized by insulin resistance. Options C and D are incorrect explanations for the primary etiology of Type 1 diabetes.
</details>

---

### Question 212
A nurse is assessing a client who has a history of diabetes mellitus. Which of the following findings is an expected clinical manifestation of hypoglycemia?

A) Deep, rapid respirations
B) Hot, dry, and flushed skin
C) Tremors, diaphoresis, and tachycardia
D) Increased thirst and frequent urination

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Expected vs Unexpected
**System Focus:** Hypoglycemia

**Rationale:** Hypoglycemia (low blood sugar) triggers the release of epinephrine, causing expected sympathetic nervous system symptoms such as tremors, sweating (diaphoresis), palpitations, and tachycardia. Options A, B, and D are expected manifestations of hyperglycemia (high blood sugar), such as seen in diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) or hyperosmolar hyperglycemic state (HHS).
</details>

### Question 213
A nurse is preparing to administer the daily dose of levothyroxine to a client diagnosed with hypothyroidism. Which action is the most appropriate procedural step for administering this medication?

A) Administer the medication 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast with a full glass of water.
B) Give the medication in the evening with a high-protein dinner to enhance absorption.
C) Administer the medication immediately after lunch alongside a calcium supplement.
D) Crush the medication and mix it in a small cup of milk for easier swallowing.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Procedural Step
**System Focus:** Thyroid medication

**Rationale:** Levothyroxine should be administered on an empty stomach, ideally 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast with a full glass of water, to ensure optimal and consistent absorption. Giving it with food, especially calcium, iron, or milk (Options B, C, and D), significantly decreases the absorption of the medication.
</details>

### Question 214
The nurse reviews the blood glucose log of a client with Type 2 diabetes mellitus. The client's capillary blood glucose reading at 1400 is 52 mg/dL. How should the nurse interpret this data?

A) The client is experiencing severe hyperglycemia and needs immediate insulin.
B) The client's blood glucose is within the normal and expected target range.
C) The client is at immediate risk for developing diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA).
D) The client is experiencing hypoglycemia and requires a fast-acting carbohydrate.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Data Interpretation
**System Focus:** Hypoglycemia

**Rationale:** A blood glucose level below 70 mg/dL indicates hypoglycemia. The nurse should correctly interpret this data as hypoglycemia, which requires immediate treatment with 15 grams of a fast-acting carbohydrate (such as fruit juice or glucose tablets) to raise the blood sugar safely. Options A, B, and C misinterpret the data, as 52 mg/dL is abnormally low, not high or normal.
</details>
