### Question 215
A client has been taking levothyroxine for hypothyroidism for the past three months. Which statement by the client indicates to the nurse that the medication is having the desired therapeutic effect?

A) "I have noticed my resting heart rate is constantly over 110 beats per minute."
B) "I have much more energy during the day and have lost a few pounds recently."
C) "I am still feeling very cold all the time, even when the house is warm."
D) "I have been experiencing severe tremors in my hands when I try to write."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Evaluation
**System Focus:** Thyroid medication

**Rationale:** Levothyroxine is a synthetic thyroid hormone used to treat hypothyroidism. A therapeutic response includes the reversal of hypothyroid symptoms, such as an increase in energy levels, improved metabolism (weight loss), and normal temperature tolerance. Options A and D are signs of hyperthyroidism, indicating the dose may be too high (toxicity). Option C indicates the medication is not yet effective or the dose is too low.
</details>
### Question 216
A client with diabetes mellitus is sweating, trembling, and reports feeling dizzy. The nurse checks the client's capillary blood glucose level. Which laboratory value clearly confirms the client is experiencing hypoglycemia?

A) 105 mg/dL
B) 88 mg/dL
C) 62 mg/dL
D) 140 mg/dL

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) 62 mg/dL

**Rationale:** 
- **Data Interpretation Lens:** Hypoglycemia is generally defined as a blood glucose level below 70 mg/dL. A reading of 62 mg/dL combined with symptoms like sweating, trembling, and dizziness confirms hypoglycemia. 
- Values of 88 mg/dL and 105 mg/dL are within normal fasting or pre-meal ranges. 
- A value of 140 mg/dL is a normal post-meal level or indicates mild hyperglycemia depending on the timing of the last meal, not hypoglycemia.
</details>

### Question 217
A nurse is reinforcing teaching with a client who has a new prescription for levothyroxine to treat hypothyroidism. Which instruction should the nurse include in the teaching?

A) Mix the medication in a glass of milk at bedtime.
B) Take the medication in the morning on an empty stomach.
C) Take the medication with a high-protein dinner.
D) Take the medication only on days when feeling fatigued.

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Take the medication in the morning on an empty stomach.

**Rationale:** 
- **Client Teaching Lens:** Levothyroxine is a synthetic thyroid hormone. For optimal absorption, it must be taken once daily in the morning on an empty stomach, at least 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast. 
- Taking it at bedtime or with meals (especially high-protein or calcium-rich milk) severely decreases its absorption. 
- It is a daily maintenance medication and should never be taken "as needed" based on fatigue.
</details>

---

### Question 218
A nurse is caring for a newly admitted client who has Type 1 diabetes mellitus. Which of the following findings is an expected characteristic of this specific type of diabetes rather than Type 2?

A) The client's cells are highly resistant to the insulin being produced.
B) The condition is primarily managed with oral hypoglycemic agents.
C) The disease commonly develops as a direct result of obesity and physical inactivity.
D) The pancreas produces absolutely no endogenous insulin.

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) The pancreas produces absolutely no endogenous insulin.

**Rationale:** 
- **Expected vs Unexpected Lens:** Type 1 diabetes is characterized by the autoimmune destruction of pancreatic beta cells, resulting in an absolute lack of insulin production. Therefore, these clients require exogenous insulin for life.
- Insulin resistance (cells not responding to insulin), management with oral agents, and a strong correlation with obesity and physical inactivity are all expected characteristics of Type 2 diabetes, not Type 1.
</details>

---

### Question 219
A nurse finds an awake and alert client with Type 1 diabetes who has a blood glucose level of 55 mg/dL. The nurse plans to initiate the "Rule of 15" for hypoglycemia management. What is the first procedural step the nurse should take?

A) Recheck the capillary blood glucose level in 15 minutes.
B) Administer 15 grams of a simple, fast-acting carbohydrate.
C) Administer 1 mg of glucagon intramuscularly.
D) Provide a complex carbohydrate and protein snack.

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Administer 15 grams of a simple, fast-acting carbohydrate.

**Rationale:** 
- **Procedural Step Lens:** For a conscious, alert client experiencing hypoglycemia, the first step in the "Rule of 15" is to administer 15 grams of a fast-acting carbohydrate (such as 4 oz of fruit juice or regular soda). 
- Rechecking the blood sugar happens 15 minutes *after* administering the carbohydrate. 
- A complex carbohydrate and protein snack is given *after* the blood sugar has stabilized to prevent a subsequent drop. 
- Glucagon is reserved for clients who are unconscious or unable to swallow safely.
</details>

---

### Question 220
A client has been taking levothyroxine for hypothyroidism for the past three months. Which client statement indicates to the nurse that the medication is having the desired therapeutic effect?

A) "I feel much more energetic and no longer feel cold all the time."
B) "I have noticed my heart is racing constantly now."
C) "I have gained 10 pounds since my last clinic visit."
D) "I am having severe trouble falling asleep at night."

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) "I feel much more energetic and no longer feel cold all the time."

**Rationale:** 
- **Evaluation Lens:** Hypothyroidism slows down the body's metabolism, causing symptoms like fatigue, cold intolerance, and weight gain. An effective dose of levothyroxine will reverse these symptoms, leading to improved energy and normal body temperature regulation.
- A racing heart and insomnia are adverse effects indicating the dose may be too high (causing hyperthyroidism). 
- Continued weight gain indicates the hypothyroidism is not yet adequately managed.
</details>
### Question 221
**Lens:** Client Teaching
A young adult newly diagnosed with Type 1 diabetes asks the nurse, "Why do I have to take shots? My grandfather has diabetes and only takes pills." Which is the best response by the nurse?

A) "Pills are only effective for older adults who have had diabetes for many years."
B) "In Type 1 diabetes, your body does not make insulin, so it must be replaced with injections."
C) "You will eventually be able to switch to pills once your blood sugar is under control."
D) "Pills are specifically used when a patient is overweight and needs to lose fat."

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune condition characterized by an absolute lack of insulin production due to the destruction of pancreatic beta cells. Because the body makes no insulin, exogenous insulin (via injections or a pump) is required to sustain life. Oral antidiabetic agents (pills) are primarily used in Type 2 diabetes, a condition where the body still produces some insulin but experiences insulin resistance.
</details>

---

### Question 222
**Lens:** Procedural Step
A conscious client with diabetes reports feeling shaky, weak, and sweaty. A capillary blood glucose test reveals a level of 55 mg/dL. What is the nurse's immediate action?

A) Administer 15 grams of a fast-acting oral carbohydrate.
B) Inject 1 mg of glucagon intramuscularly.
C) Provide a high-protein, high-fat meal like a cheeseburger.
D) Administer the client's scheduled dose of regular insulin.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** For a conscious client experiencing mild to moderate hypoglycemia (blood glucose < 70 mg/dL), the standard procedural step is to follow the "Rule of 15": administer 15 grams of a fast-acting carbohydrate (e.g., 4 oz of fruit juice, regular soda, or glucose tablets), wait 15 minutes, and recheck the blood glucose. Glucagon is reserved for clients who are unconscious or unable to swallow safely. High-fat foods slow the absorption of glucose, delaying recovery. Administering more insulin would dangerously worsen the hypoglycemia.
</details>

---

### Question 223
**Lens:** Evaluation
A client has been taking levothyroxine for primary hypothyroidism for three months. Which client statement indicates to the nurse that the medication is effective?

A) "I have gained about 10 pounds since my last visit."
B) "I still need to wear a heavy sweater inside the house."
C) "I have noticed that I have much more energy during the day."
D) "My heart feels like it is pounding rapidly when I sit down."

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** Hypothyroidism causes a slowed metabolic rate, resulting in fatigue, lethargy, weight gain, and cold intolerance. An improvement in energy levels indicates that the levothyroxine (synthetic thyroid hormone) is effectively increasing the metabolic rate back to normal. Continued weight gain and cold intolerance indicate undertreatment. A rapidly pounding heart (tachycardia) is a sign of hyperthyroidism, which could indicate a medication overdose.
</details>

---

### Question 224
**Lens:** Delegation
The registered nurse (RN) is planning care for a client with Type 2 diabetes. Which task is appropriate to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Assess the client's bilateral heels and toes for newly formed diabetic ulcers.
B) Teach the client how to properly clean the finger before using the lancet.
C) Calculate the correct sliding-scale insulin dose based on the lunch tray.
D) Deliver a bedtime snack of graham crackers and milk to the client's room.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Rationale:** UAPs can safely perform standard, non-invasive, routine tasks such as delivering meals or prescribed snacks to a client's room. Assessing skin integrity, providing patient education, and utilizing clinical judgment to calculate medication dosages all fall strictly under the RN scope of practice and cannot be delegated to a UAP.
</details>

---

### Question 225
**Lens:** Contraindication
A client is prescribed daily levothyroxine for the treatment of hypothyroidism. Which action by the client would be contraindicated because it decreases the absorption of the medication?

A) Swallowing the medication with a full glass of water.
B) Taking the medication simultaneously with an over-the-counter calcium supplement.
C) Taking the pill on an empty stomach 30 minutes before eating breakfast.
D) Taking the medication at the exact same time every morning.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** Levothyroxine absorption is significantly decreased by calcium supplements, iron supplements, and antacids. Therefore, taking the medication at the same time as a calcium supplement is contraindicated. The client should be taught to separate levothyroxine from these supplements by at least 4 hours. Taking it with water, on an empty stomach before breakfast, and at the same time daily are all expected and correct administration practices.
</details>
### Question 226
A nurse is teaching a community health class about diabetes mellitus. Which statement by a participant accurately describes the fundamental basis of Type 1 diabetes?

A. "It is usually treated with diet and exercise before starting pills."
B. "The pancreas produces no insulin, requiring lifelong insulin therapy."
C. "It primarily occurs due to obesity and cellular insulin resistance."
D. "This type of diabetes typically only occurs in older adults."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Lens</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. "The pancreas produces no insulin, requiring lifelong insulin therapy."

**Rationale:** Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune condition where the beta cells in the pancreas are destroyed, leading to an absolute lack of insulin production. Therefore, clients with Type 1 diabetes require lifelong exogenous insulin therapy. Options A, C, and D describe Type 2 diabetes, which is characterized by insulin resistance, is strongly associated with obesity and lifestyle factors, typically develops in adulthood (though pediatric cases are rising), and is initially managed with diet, exercise, and oral antidiabetic medications. 

**Lens:** Client Teaching
</details>

---

### Question 227
A client with diabetes mellitus is awake, alert, and reports feeling suddenly shaky, sweaty, and anxious. The nurse checks the client's capillary blood glucose and obtains a reading of 58 mg/dL (3.2 mmol/L). What is the most appropriate procedural step for the nurse to take?

A. Administer 1 mg of glucagon intramuscularly.
B. Provide a high-protein snack like cheese and crackers immediately.
C. Give the client 4 ounces of regular fruit juice or soda.
D. Recheck the blood glucose in 1 hour without intervention.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Lens</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. Give the client 4 ounces of regular fruit juice or soda.

**Rationale:** The client is experiencing hypoglycemia (blood glucose < 70 mg/dL) but is awake and alert. The standard "Rule of 15" for conscious hypoglycemia is to provide 15 grams of simple, fast-acting carbohydrates, such as 4 ounces of regular fruit juice or soda. Glucagon (Option A) is reserved for clients who are unconscious or unable to swallow safely. A high-protein snack (Option B) delays the absorption of carbohydrates and should only be given *after* the blood glucose has stabilized. Waiting 1 hour (Option D) is dangerous and can lead to severe hypoglycemia and loss of consciousness.

**Lens:** Procedural Step
</details>

---

### Question 228
A client with hypothyroidism has been taking a daily dose of levothyroxine for the past three months. Which assessment finding indicates to the nurse that the medication is having the expected therapeutic effect?

A. The client reports having more energy and improved mood.
B. The client's resting heart rate is consistently 115 beats per minute.
C. The client complains of feeling cold all the time despite wearing warm clothing.
D. The client reports increased lethargy and recent, unexplained weight gain.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Lens</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. The client reports having more energy and improved mood.

**Rationale:** Levothyroxine is a synthetic thyroid hormone used to treat hypothyroidism. Expected therapeutic effects include the reversal of hypothyroid symptoms, which means the client should experience increased energy, normalized weight, improved mood, and normalized temperature tolerance. Lethargy, feeling cold, and weight gain (Options C and D) are symptoms of uncontrolled hypothyroidism, indicating the dose may be too low. A heart rate of 115 bpm (Option B) is tachycardia, which is an unexpected adverse effect indicating hyperthyroidism (the levothyroxine dose may be too high).

**Lens:** Expected vs Unexpected
</details>

---

### Question 229
The nurse is assessing a group of adult clients during a health fair screening. Which client should the nurse identify as having the highest risk for developing Type 2 diabetes mellitus?

A. A 25-year-old with a history of recurrent viral infections.
B. A 30-year-old who consumes a strict vegetarian diet.
C. A 50-year-old with a family history of autoimmune thyroiditis.
D. A 45-year-old who is overweight and leads a sedentary lifestyle.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Lens</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. A 45-year-old who is overweight and leads a sedentary lifestyle.

**Rationale:** Major risk factors for developing Type 2 diabetes mellitus include being overweight or obese, physical inactivity (sedentary lifestyle), advancing age (typically over 35-40), and a family history of Type 2 diabetes. Autoimmune conditions and recurrent viral infections (Options A and C) are sometimes theorized as triggers for Type 1 diabetes, not Type 2. A vegetarian diet (Option B) generally promotes metabolic health and does not independently increase diabetes risk.

**Lens:** Risk Recognition
</details>

---

### Question 230
The registered nurse (RN) is caring for a client with Type 2 diabetes who is scheduled to receive a rapid-acting insulin injection before dinner. Which task is appropriate for the RN to delegate to unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A. Distributing the client's evening meal tray so it is available when the insulin is given.
B. Assessing the client for signs of early evening hypoglycemia.
C. Educating the client on how to rotate injection sites.
D. Evaluating the client's understanding of their prescribed diabetic diet.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Lens</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. Distributing the client's evening meal tray so it is available when the insulin is given.

**Rationale:** The scope of practice for unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) includes routine, standard tasks that do not require nursing judgment, such as delivering meal trays, assisting with feeding, and basic hygiene. Because rapid-acting insulin requires food to be present to prevent hypoglycemia, delegating the delivery of the meal tray is highly appropriate and safe. Assessing for hypoglycemia, educating the client, and evaluating the client's understanding (Options B, C, and D) all require clinical nursing judgment and fall exclusively within the RN's scope of practice.

**Lens:** Delegation
</details>
### Question 231
**Lens:** Client Teaching
A nurse is educating a newly diagnosed client about the difference between Type 1 and Type 2 diabetes mellitus. Which statement by the nurse is the most accurate regarding the basics of these conditions?

A. "Type 1 diabetes occurs over time when your body becomes resistant to the insulin it produces."
B. "In Type 2 diabetes, the pancreas completely stops producing insulin, requiring immediate injections."
C. "Type 1 diabetes means your pancreas no longer makes insulin, so you will need insulin injections for life."
D. "Type 2 diabetes is a temporary condition that goes away completely once you start eating a healthy diet."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** Type 1 diabetes is characterized by an absolute lack of insulin production due to the autoimmune destruction of pancreatic beta cells, requiring lifelong exogenous insulin. Type 2 diabetes is primarily characterized by cellular insulin resistance and a relative insulin deficiency, often managed with diet, exercise, oral medications, and sometimes insulin.
</details>

---

### Question 232
**Lens:** Procedural Step
A conscious client with Type 1 diabetes calls the nurse and reports feeling shaky, weak, and extremely sweaty. A capillary blood glucose test reveals a level of 54 mg/dL. What is the nurse's immediate next step?

A. Administer 15 grams of a fast-acting oral carbohydrate.
B. Administer a subcutaneous injection of regular insulin.
C. Prepare to administer an intramuscular injection of glucagon.
D. Wait fifteen minutes and recheck the client's blood glucose level.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** For a conscious client experiencing mild to moderate hypoglycemia (blood glucose < 70 mg/dL), the proper procedural step is to implement the "Rule of 15": administer 15 grams of a fast-acting carbohydrate (such as 4 oz of juice or regular soda) and recheck the blood glucose in 15 minutes. Glucagon is reserved for clients who are unconscious or unable to swallow.
</details>

---

### Question 233
**Lens:** Evaluation
A client has been taking levothyroxine for primary hypothyroidism for the past three months. Which finding best indicates to the nurse that the medication is having the desired therapeutic effect?

A. The client reports a recent, unintended weight gain of 8 lbs.
B. The client states they have more energy and tolerate cold environments better.
C. The client's resting heart rate is consistently measured at 115 beats per minute.
D. The client complains of frequent episodes of diarrhea and heat intolerance.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** Levothyroxine is a synthetic thyroid hormone used to treat hypothyroidism. Therapeutic evaluation includes the resolution of hypothyroid symptoms. Increased energy and improved cold tolerance are expected positive outcomes. Weight gain is a sign of continuing hypothyroidism, while a heart rate of 115 bpm, diarrhea, and heat intolerance are signs of hyperthyroidism (indicating the medication dose may be too high).
</details>

---

### Question 234
**Lens:** Data Interpretation
The nurse is reviewing the classic signs and symptoms of endocrine imbalances. Which of the following client assessment findings should the nurse interpret as a classic sign of hypoglycemia?

A. Deep, rapid respirations
B. Flushed, hot, and dry skin
C. Increased urine output
D. Cold, clammy skin

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** D

**Rationale:** Diaphoresis resulting in cold, clammy skin is a classic autonomic symptom of hypoglycemia. A common nursing mnemonic is "Cold and clammy, need some candy." The other symptoms listed—Kussmaul respirations (deep, rapid breathing), hot/dry skin, and polyuria (increased urine output)—are associated with hyperglycemia and diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA).
</details>

---

### Question 235
**Lens:** Delegation
The registered nurse (RN) is caring for a stable client with Type 2 diabetes who is scheduled for a capillary blood glucose check before lunch. Which task can the RN safely delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A. Determine the client's lunch-time sliding-scale insulin dose based on the reading.
B. Perform the capillary blood glucose fingerstick and record the result in the chart.
C. Assess the client's understanding of their prescribed diabetic carbohydrate-controlled diet.
D. Provide education to the client on how to properly rotate insulin injection sites.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** Performing a routine capillary blood glucose fingerstick and recording the value is a standard, repeatable task that falls safely within the scope of practice for a UAP. Assessing understanding, providing medical education, and determining medication dosages are strictly nursing responsibilities that cannot be delegated.
</details>
### Question 236
**Lens: Client Teaching**

A nurse is teaching a newly diagnosed adult client about the pathophysiology of Type 1 diabetes mellitus. Which statement should the nurse include in the teaching?

A) "Your pancreas no longer produces insulin, so you will need insulin injections for life."
B) "You can likely manage your condition with just strict diet and daily exercise."
C) "This type of diabetes is primarily caused by eating too much sugar over the years."
D) "We will start you on oral medications to stimulate your pancreas to make more insulin."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** Type 1 diabetes is characterized by an autoimmune destruction of the beta cells in the pancreas, leading to an absolute deficiency of insulin. Because the body can no longer produce insulin, clients with Type 1 diabetes require lifelong exogenous insulin replacement. Options B and D describe treatments for Type 2 diabetes. Option C is a common misconception; Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune condition, not a direct result of dietary choices.
</details>

---

### Question 237
**Lens: Procedural Step**

A nurse is instructing a client on the proper procedure for taking their prescribed levothyroxine for hypothyroidism. Which instruction demonstrates the correct administration step?

A) "Take the medication right before going to bed with a glass of milk."
B) "Take the medication with breakfast to prevent stomach upset."
C) "Take the medication in the morning on an empty stomach, 30 to 60 minutes before eating."
D) "Take the medication with your daily antacid to improve absorption."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** Levothyroxine should be taken first thing in the morning on an empty stomach, at least 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast. This ensures optimal and consistent absorption of the medication. Food, milk, and certain medications (like antacids containing calcium or aluminum) can significantly decrease the absorption of levothyroxine, making options A, B, and D incorrect.
</details>

---

### Question 238
**Lens: Expected vs Unexpected**

A nurse is assessing a client with Type 2 diabetes whose current capillary blood glucose level is 52 mg/dL. Which clinical manifestation is an expected finding for this blood glucose level?

A) Deep, rapid breathing and fruity breath
B) Tremors, diaphoresis, and palpitations
C) Hot, dry skin and excessive thirst
D) Bradycardia and increased urinary output

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** A blood glucose level of 52 mg/dL indicates hypoglycemia (low blood sugar). Expected signs of hypoglycemia include tremors, diaphoresis (sweating), palpitations, tachycardia, and irritability due to the release of epinephrine. Deep, rapid breathing (Kussmaul respirations) and fruity breath (Option A), as well as hot, dry skin and excessive thirst (Option C), are expected findings in hyperglycemia or diabetic ketoacidosis. Bradycardia and increased urinary output (Option D) are not associated with hypoglycemia.
</details>

---

### Question 239
**Lens: Delegation**

A nurse is caring for a client who is recovering from a mild hypoglycemic episode. Which task is most appropriate for the nurse to safely delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Assess the client's understanding of what caused the low blood sugar.
B) Determine if the client needs an adjustment in their morning insulin dose.
C) Check the client's gag reflex before offering them a snack.
D) Provide the client with a complex carbohydrate and protein snack as directed.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** Providing a snack to a stable client is a routine, non-invasive task that falls within the UAP's scope of practice. Assessing a client's understanding (Option A), evaluating the need for medication adjustments (Option B), and assessing physiological functions like a gag reflex (Option C) require nursing judgment and cannot be delegated to a UAP. 
</details>

---

### Question 240
**Lens: Evaluation**

A nurse has provided education to a client newly diagnosed with Type 2 diabetes. Which statement by the client indicates that the teaching was successful?

A) "I developed this condition because my body is completely destroying its own insulin-producing cells."
B) "I will need to check my ketones every morning to prevent diabetic ketoacidosis."
C) "Because I have Type 2, I will never need to take insulin injections."
D) "My cells are resisting the insulin my body makes, but weight loss and exercise can help improve this."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** Type 2 diabetes is primarily characterized by insulin resistance, where the body produces insulin but the cells do not use it effectively. Lifestyle modifications such as weight loss, exercise, and diet can significantly improve insulin sensitivity. Option A describes Type 1 diabetes. Option B is incorrect because routine ketone testing is generally for Type 1 diabetes, not Type 2. Option C is incorrect because many clients with Type 2 diabetes eventually require insulin therapy as the disease progresses.
</details>
### Question 241
A nurse is educating a client newly diagnosed with Type 1 diabetes mellitus. Which statement by the nurse accurately describes the primary cause of this condition? 

A) "Your pancreas produces too much insulin, which causes blood sugar to drop."
B) "Your body's cells have become resistant to the insulin your pancreas makes."
C) "Your pancreas no longer produces insulin because the insulin-producing cells were destroyed."
D) "Your liver is releasing too much glucose into your bloodstream after you eat."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Client Teaching
**Rationale:** Type 1 diabetes mellitus is an autoimmune condition where the body's immune system destroys the beta cells in the pancreas. As a result, the pancreas produces little to no insulin. Option B describes the pathophysiology of Type 2 diabetes, which is characterized by cellular insulin resistance. Option A and D do not accurately describe the primary mechanism of Type 1 diabetes.
</details>

---

### Question 242
A client with diabetes receives a dose of regular insulin before breakfast but then refuses to eat. Which finding should the nurse identify as an unexpected consequence requiring immediate intervention?

A) Diaphoresis and shakiness
B) Warm, dry skin and excessive thirst
C) A blood glucose reading of 105 mg/dL
D) Reports of generalized fatigue and polyuria

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Expected vs Unexpected
**Rationale:** Administering short-acting (regular) insulin to a client who does not eat predictably leads to a dangerous drop in blood glucose. Diaphoresis (sweating), shakiness, tremors, and tachycardia are classic signs of hypoglycemia, which is an unexpected and adverse outcome that must be treated immediately. Options B and D are signs of hyperglycemia. Option C is a normal blood glucose reading. 
</details>

---

### Question 243
A nurse is instructing a client on the proper administration of a newly prescribed levothyroxine daily dose for hypothyroidism. Which procedural step should the nurse include in the instructions?

A) Take the medication with a high-calcium meal to enhance absorption.
B) Administer the pill right before going to bed at night.
C) Take the medication with antacids if stomach upset occurs.
D) Swallow the pill with a full glass of water on an empty stomach 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Procedural Step
**Rationale:** Levothyroxine must be taken on an empty stomach, typically first thing in the morning, 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast or drinking caffeinated beverages. Food, calcium, iron supplements, and antacids significantly decrease the absorption of the medication, making options A and C incorrect. Option B is incorrect because taking it at night or with evening meals can disrupt sleep and alter absorption.
</details>

---

### Question 244
A nurse is reviewing the capillary blood glucose log for a client with Type 2 diabetes. The client reports waking up feeling dizzy, confused, and having a rapid heartbeat. The morning blood glucose reading is 52 mg/dL. How should the nurse interpret these data?

A) The client is experiencing early signs of diabetic ketoacidosis.
B) The client is experiencing hypoglycemia.
C) The client's blood glucose is within the target fasting range.
D) The client is experiencing a hyperosmolar hyperglycemic state.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Data Interpretation
**Rationale:** A blood glucose reading below 70 mg/dL is classified as hypoglycemia. The client's symptoms of dizziness, confusion, and a rapid heartbeat (tachycardia) perfectly align with the data interpretation of a hypoglycemic episode. Diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) and hyperosmolar hyperglycemic state (HHS) are complications of severe hyperglycemia, not hypoglycemia. 
</details>

---

### Question 245
A nurse is caring for a client with poorly controlled Type 2 diabetes mellitus. Which lifestyle factor should the nurse recognize as the highest risk for exacerbating insulin resistance in this client?

A) Drinking 2 to 3 liters of water per day.
B) Sleeping 7 to 8 hours a night.
C) Consuming a diet high in lean proteins and green vegetables.
D) Leading a sedentary lifestyle with a high-carbohydrate diet.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Risk Recognition
**Rationale:** Type 2 diabetes is heavily influenced by lifestyle factors. A sedentary lifestyle and a diet high in carbohydrates and unhealthy fats promote weight gain and directly exacerbate cellular insulin resistance, which is the hallmark issue in Type 2 diabetes. Options A, B, and C are healthy lifestyle choices that would help manage or prevent complications of diabetes.
</details>
### Question 246
**Lens: Client Teaching**

A nurse is educating a newly diagnosed adult client about the primary difference between type 1 and type 2 diabetes mellitus. Which statement by the nurse is the most accurate?

A. "Type 1 diabetes occurs when your pancreas produces no insulin, while type 2 happens when your body resists the insulin it does produce."
B. "Type 1 diabetes is usually managed with diet and exercise alone, while type 2 always requires lifetime insulin shots."
C. "Type 2 diabetes only occurs in young children, whereas type 1 usually begins in older adulthood."
D. "Type 1 diabetes means your body produces too much insulin, which causes your blood sugar levels to drop too low."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune condition in which the beta cells of the pancreas are destroyed, leading to an absolute lack of insulin production. Type 2 diabetes primarily involves insulin resistance, where the body produces insulin but the cells do not use it effectively, often accompanied by a gradual decline in insulin production over time. Options B, C, and D contain incorrect statements regarding the pathophysiology, typical onset, and management of the two types of diabetes.
</details>

---

### Question 247
**Lens: Procedural Step**

A nurse finds a conscious client with type 1 diabetes shaking, sweating, and reporting a mild headache. A capillary blood glucose test reads 58 mg/dL. What is the correct next step for the nurse to take?

A. Administer 1 mg of glucagon intramuscularly.
B. Provide 15 grams of a fast-acting oral carbohydrate.
C. Inject 10 units of regular insulin subcutaneously.
D. Offer the client a high-fat snack, such as a slice of pizza.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** Following the "Rule of 15" for a conscious client experiencing mild to moderate hypoglycemia (blood glucose less than 70 mg/dL), the nurse should immediately provide 15 grams of a fast-acting oral carbohydrate (such as 4 ounces of fruit juice or regular soda). Intramuscular glucagon (Option A) is reserved for clients who are unconscious or unable to swallow. Insulin (Option C) would further lower the blood glucose and is contraindicated. High-fat foods (Option D) delay gastric emptying and slow the absorption of carbohydrates, making them a poor choice for quickly correcting hypoglycemia.
</details>

---

### Question 248
**Lens: Expected vs Unexpected**

A client has been taking levothyroxine for hypothyroidism for the past three months. During a clinic visit, the nurse evaluates the effectiveness of the medication. Which finding indicates an expected therapeutic response to the drug?

A. The client reports increased lethargy and sleeping 12 hours a day.
B. The client's resting heart rate is consistently 115 beats per minute.
C. The client reports an improvement in energy levels and regular bowel movements.
D. The client has experienced an unexplained weight gain of 10 pounds over two weeks.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** Levothyroxine is a synthetic thyroid hormone used to treat hypothyroidism. A therapeutic response includes the resolution of hypothyroid symptoms, such as improved energy levels, normalized weight, and regular bowel habits (relieving constipation). Lethargy and weight gain (Options A and D) indicate undertreatment or an ineffective dose. Tachycardia (Option B) is an unexpected finding that indicates potential overtreatment, pushing the client into a hyperthyroid state.
</details>

---

### Question 249
**Lens: Data Interpretation**

The nurse is reviewing the signs of hypoglycemia with a group of nursing students. Which combination of physical manifestations should the students correctly interpret as classic indicators of a low blood glucose level?

A. Extreme thirst, frequent urination, and fruity-smelling breath.
B. High fever, bradycardia, and pinpoint pupils.
C. Deep, rapid breathing and flushed, dry skin.
D. Diaphoresis, tremors, and confusion.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** D

**Rationale:** Classic signs of hypoglycemia are triggered by the release of epinephrine and a lack of glucose to the brain. These include diaphoresis (sweating), tremors, tachycardia, palpitations, confusion, irritability, and hunger. Extreme thirst, frequent urination, fruity breath, deep/rapid breathing (Kussmaul respirations), and flushed dry skin (Options A and C) are signs of hyperglycemia or diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA). Option B does not describe hypoglycemia.
</details>

---

### Question 250
**Lens: Delegation**

The registered nurse (RN) is caring for a client with type 2 diabetes who requires blood glucose monitoring and morning insulin. Which task is appropriate for the RN to delegate to the Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP)?

A. Administer the prescribed subcutaneous regular insulin dose.
B. Obtain the client's capillary blood glucose level before breakfast.
C. Teach the client how to properly rotate insulin injection sites.
D. Assess the client's knowledge of the symptoms of hypoglycemia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** Obtaining a capillary blood glucose level is a standard, routine procedure that is within the scope of practice for a trained UAP. Administering medication (Option A), providing client education (Option C), and performing nursing assessments (Option D) require the specialized knowledge and clinical judgment of a licensed nurse and cannot be delegated to a UAP.
</details>
### Question 251
**Lens:** Client Teaching
A nurse is teaching a client newly diagnosed with type 1 diabetes mellitus. Which statement by the client indicates an accurate understanding of the disease process?

A. "I can switch to oral diabetes pills once I lose enough weight."
B. "My pancreas no longer produces insulin, so I will need injections."
C. "I only need to check my blood sugar when I feel dizzy or sick."
D. "This condition is caused by my body becoming resistant to the insulin it makes."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

*   **Correct Answer: B.** Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune condition where the beta cells of the pancreas are destroyed, leading to an absolute deficiency of insulin. Clients with type 1 diabetes require lifelong insulin therapy (via injections or pump).
*   **Option A is incorrect.** Oral antidiabetic medications are used for type 2 diabetes, not type 1. Weight loss does not restore insulin production in type 1 diabetes.
*   **Option C is incorrect.** Clients with type 1 diabetes must monitor their blood glucose regularly (multiple times a day), not just when feeling unwell.
*   **Option D is incorrect.** Insulin resistance is the primary mechanism of type 2 diabetes, whereas type 1 diabetes is characterized by an absolute lack of insulin.
</details>

---

### Question 252
**Lens:** Procedural Step
A client with type 2 diabetes reports feeling shaky and sweating profusely. The client's capillary blood glucose is 58 mg/dL. The client is awake, alert, and able to swallow. What procedural step should the nurse take next?

A. Administer 1 mg of glucagon intramuscularly.
B. Instruct the client to eat a high-protein meal immediately.
C. Give the client a subcutaneous injection of rapid-acting insulin.
D. Provide 4 ounces of apple juice or regular soda.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

*   **Correct Answer: D.** For a conscious client experiencing hypoglycemia (blood glucose < 70 mg/dL) who can safely swallow, the "Rule of 15" is the standard procedural step. The nurse should provide 15 grams of fast-acting, simple carbohydrates, such as 4 ounces (120 mL) of juice or regular (non-diet) soda.
*   **Option A is incorrect.** Intramuscular glucagon is indicated for severe hypoglycemia when the client is unconscious or unable to swallow safely.
*   **Option B is incorrect.** While protein may be given *after* blood sugar has stabilized to prevent a secondary drop, it does not raise blood sugar quickly enough during an acute hypoglycemic episode.
*   **Option C is incorrect.** Administering insulin to a client who is already hypoglycemic will further dangerously lower their blood sugar.
</details>

---

### Question 253
**Lens:** Expected vs Unexpected
A client has been taking daily levothyroxine for hypothyroidism for the past six weeks. Which assessment finding should the nurse recognize as an UNEXPECTED adverse effect indicating a potential medication overdose?

A. A resting heart rate of 115 beats per minute
B. A slight decrease in the client's overall body weight
C. The client reports feeling more energetic during the day
D. The client states they are having daily formed bowel movements

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

*   **Correct Answer: A.** Levothyroxine is a synthetic thyroid hormone replacement. A dosage that is too high can cause signs of hyperthyroidism (thyrotoxicosis), including tachycardia (heart rate > 100 bpm), palpitations, heat intolerance, and tremors. This is an unexpected adverse effect that requires reporting to the provider.
*   **Option B is incorrect.** Weight loss is an expected, desired outcome of correcting hypothyroidism, as the medication increases the basal metabolic rate.
*   **Option C is incorrect.** Increased energy is an expected, therapeutic effect since hypothyroidism typically causes profound fatigue.
*   **Option D is incorrect.** Hypothyroidism commonly causes constipation. Regular, daily formed bowel movements indicate an expected improvement in gastrointestinal motility.
</details>

---

### Question 254
**Lens:** Data Interpretation
A nurse is reviewing the laboratory results for an adult client with type 2 diabetes mellitus. The client's glycosylated hemoglobin (HbA1c) level is 6.8%. How should the nurse interpret this data?

A. The client is experiencing an episode of acute, severe hypoglycemia.
B. The client has had extremely high blood sugars over the past week.
C. The client's long-term glucose management is currently at an acceptable goal.
D. The client needs an immediate administration of intravenous regular insulin.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

*   **Correct Answer: C.** The HbA1c test measures the average blood glucose level over the past 2 to 3 months. For most non-pregnant adults with diabetes, the American Diabetes Association recommends an HbA1c target of less than 7.0%. A result of 6.8% indicates acceptable long-term glycemic control.
*   **Option A is incorrect.** HbA1c does not measure acute blood sugar levels or indicate immediate hypoglycemia. Capillary or venous blood glucose testing is used for acute monitoring.
*   **Option B is incorrect.** HbA1c reflects a 2-to-3-month average, not just the past week. A level of 6.8% does not suggest "extremely high" blood sugars.
*   **Option D is incorrect.** IV regular insulin is used for acute hyperglycemic crises (like DKA or HHS). A stable HbA1c of 6.8% requires continuation of the current, effective treatment plan, not emergency intervention.
</details>

---

### Question 255
**Lens:** Delegation
A registered nurse (RN) is caring for a client with diabetes who is recovering from a mild hypoglycemic episode. The client's blood sugar is now 105 mg/dL and stable. Which task is appropriate to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A. Delivering a scheduled bedtime snack of graham crackers and milk to the client.
B. Educating the client on the early warning signs of low blood sugar.
C. Assessing the client's abdominal injection sites for lipohypertrophy.
D. Deciding if the client needs to skip their morning dose of oral diabetic medication.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

*   **Correct Answer: A.** Delivering a scheduled meal or snack to a stable client is a routine task that falls safely within the scope of practice for unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP). 
*   **Option B is incorrect.** Client education is a nursing responsibility and cannot be delegated to a UAP.
*   **Option C is incorrect.** Assessing the skin or injection sites requires nursing judgment and must be performed by the RN.
*   **Option D is incorrect.** Making clinical decisions about modifying or withholding medications is the responsibility of the RN or healthcare provider and cannot be delegated to a UAP.
</details>
### Question 256
A nurse is educating a newly diagnosed client with type 1 diabetes mellitus. Which statement by the nurse accurately describes the primary cause of this condition?

A) "Your pancreas no longer produces insulin, so you will need insulin injections for life."
B) "Your body's cells have become resistant to the insulin your pancreas is making."
C) "This condition can usually be reversed with a strict diet and daily exercise."
D) "You will only need to take insulin injections when you are sick or under high stress."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Client Teaching
**System Focus:** Type 1 vs Type 2 Basics

**Reasoning:** Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune condition where the beta cells of the pancreas are destroyed, resulting in an absolute lack of insulin production. Therefore, clients with type 1 diabetes require lifelong exogenous insulin therapy. Option B describes the pathophysiology of type 2 diabetes (insulin resistance). Option C applies to prediabetes or type 2 diabetes, which can sometimes be managed or reversed with lifestyle changes. Option D is incorrect because a type 1 diabetic always needs insulin, not just during periods of stress or illness (though requirements may increase during those times).
</details>

---

### Question 257
A nurse is assessing a client who has a capillary blood glucose level of 55 mg/dL. Which of the following physical findings should the nurse expect to observe?

A) Deep, rapid respirations
B) Flushed, dry skin
C) Cold, clammy skin and tremors
D) Increased urine output and extreme thirst

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Expected vs Unexpected
**System Focus:** Hypoglycemia

**Reasoning:** A blood glucose level of 55 mg/dL indicates hypoglycemia (normal fasting is 70-100 mg/dL). Expected signs of hypoglycemia include cold, clammy (diaphoretic) skin, tremors, tachycardia, irritability, and confusion ("cold and clammy, need some candy"). Options A, B, and D are classic signs of hyperglycemia or diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA), which include Kussmaul respirations, warm/dry skin, polyuria (frequent urination), and polydipsia (extreme thirst). 
</details>

---

### Question 258
A nurse is instructing a client on the proper administration of levothyroxine for the treatment of hypothyroidism. Which procedural instruction should the nurse include?

A) "Take the medication right before you go to bed."
B) "Take the pill with a full glass of milk or an antacid."
C) "Take the medication with your largest meal of the day."
D) "Take the pill in the morning on an empty stomach."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Procedural Step
**System Focus:** Thyroid Medication

**Reasoning:** Levothyroxine absorption is heavily influenced by food and other medications. The correct procedural step is to take the medication once daily in the morning, on an empty stomach, at least 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast. This ensures maximum and consistent absorption. Taking it with food, milk, or antacids (Options B and C) interferes with absorption. Taking it right before bed (Option A) is generally avoided as it can cause insomnia and absorption may be altered by evening meals.
</details>

---

### Question 259
A client with type 2 diabetes experiences a mild hypoglycemic episode, and the nurse has confirmed the blood glucose is 60 mg/dL. The client is awake, alert, and able to swallow. Which task can the nurse appropriately delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Assess the client's risk for aspiration before giving oral intake.
B) Provide the client with 4 ounces of apple juice.
C) Re-evaluate the client's symptoms 15 minutes after eating.
D) Educate the client on how to prevent future hypoglycemic events.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Delegation
**System Focus:** Hypoglycemia

**Reasoning:** Providing oral intake (such as a simple carbohydrate like 4 ounces of apple juice) to an awake and alert client is a basic care task that falls within the scope of practice for a UAP. Assessing swallowing ability/aspiration risk (Option A), evaluating the effectiveness of an intervention (Option C), and providing client education (Option D) all require nursing judgment and must be performed by the registered nurse (RN). 
</details>

---

### Question 260
A client has been taking levothyroxine for hypothyroidism for the past three months. Which client finding indicates to the nurse that the medication therapy is effective?

A) The client reports feeling cold all the time.
B) The client complains of extreme fatigue and sleepiness.
C) The client reports an improvement in energy levels.
D) The client exhibits a significant, continuous weight gain.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Evaluation
**System Focus:** Thyroid Medication

**Reasoning:** Hypothyroidism slows down the body's metabolism, leading to symptoms like fatigue, cold intolerance, and weight gain. Levothyroxine is a synthetic thyroid hormone used to replace the deficient hormone and restore a normal metabolic rate. An improvement in energy levels (Option C) indicates that the medication is effective. Feeling cold (Option A), extreme fatigue (Option B), and weight gain (Option D) are ongoing signs of hypothyroidism, which would indicate that the current medication dosage is *not* yet effective.
</details>
### Question 261
A newly diagnosed client with Type 1 diabetes asks the nurse, "Why do I have to take these insulin shots? My father has Type 2 diabetes and he only takes a pill." Which response by the nurse is the most appropriate?

A) "Oral medications only work if you are overweight and need to diet."
B) "Your pancreas no longer makes insulin, so you must replace it with injections."
C) "Type 1 diabetes causes severe insulin resistance that pills cannot fix."
D) "Injections work much faster than pills to lower your blood sugar."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Client Teaching
**System Focus:** Type 1 vs Type 2 basics

Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune condition in which the body destroys the beta cells of the pancreas, leading to an absolute deficiency of insulin. Because the body makes no insulin, it must be replaced exogenously via injections or a pump. Type 2 diabetes often involves insulin resistance and a relative lack of insulin, which can frequently be managed with lifestyle changes and oral medications that help the body use its own insulin more effectively. Option A is incorrect because weight is not the sole factor for oral medications. Option C describes Type 2 diabetes. Option D is not the primary reason for requiring injections in Type 1 diabetes.
</details>

---

### Question 262
A client has been taking levothyroxine for primary hypothyroidism for the past three months. Which assessment finding indicates to the nurse that the medication is having its expected therapeutic effect?

A) The client reports an increase in cold intolerance.
B) The client's heart rate is 115 beats per minute at rest.
C) The client states they have more energy and are experiencing less fatigue.
D) The client has gained 6 pounds over the last month.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Expected vs Unexpected
**System Focus:** Thyroid medication

Levothyroxine is a synthetic thyroid hormone used to treat hypothyroidism. Expected therapeutic effects include a reversal of hypothyroid symptoms, resulting in increased energy, decreased fatigue, improved tolerance to cold, and potential weight loss (as the metabolism speeds up to a normal rate). Cold intolerance (Option A) and weight gain (Option D) are signs that the hypothyroidism is still not adequately treated. A resting heart rate of 115 bpm (Option B) is an unexpected, adverse finding that suggests hyperthyroidism from too high of a dose.
</details>

---

### Question 263
A nurse finds a conscious client with diabetes sweating profusely, trembling, and appearing confused. The nurse performs a capillary blood glucose check and gets a reading of 55 mg/dL. What is the appropriate next step for the nurse to take?

A) Give 1 mg of glucagon intramuscularly.
B) Provide a high-protein snack, such as cheese and crackers.
C) Administer a bolus of regular insulin subcutaneously.
D) Administer 15 grams of a fast-acting oral carbohydrate.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Procedural Step
**System Focus:** Hypoglycemia

The client is experiencing symptomatic hypoglycemia (blood glucose < 70 mg/dL). For a conscious client who can safely swallow, the standard procedural step is the "15-15 Rule": administer 15 grams of a fast-acting simple carbohydrate (such as 4 ounces of fruit juice or regular soda, or glucose tablets), and recheck the blood sugar in 15 minutes. Glucagon (Option A) is reserved for unconscious clients or those unable to swallow. A high-protein snack (Option B) is given *after* the blood sugar is stabilized to prevent a subsequent drop. Insulin (Option C) would dangerously lower the blood sugar further.
</details>

---

### Question 264
The registered nurse (RN) is caring for a stable client with Type 2 diabetes. Which task is appropriate for the RN to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Performing a routine capillary blood glucose check before lunch.
B) Assessing the client's feet for new signs of peripheral neuropathy.
C) Evaluating the client's understanding of their prescribed diabetic diet.
D) Administering the client's oral glipizide 30 minutes before the meal.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Delegation
**System Focus:** Type 1 vs Type 2 basics

Delegation to a UAP involves routine, standard tasks for stable clients that do not require nursing judgment. Performing a routine capillary blood glucose check is a standard skill that falls within the UAP's scope of practice in most facilities. Assessing for neuropathy (Option B), evaluating teaching or learning (Option C), and administering medications (Option D) all require nursing judgment, assessment, or licensure, and must be performed by the RN (or an LPN/LVN for medication administration, depending on state practice acts).
</details>

---

### Question 265
The nurse is reviewing the laboratory results for a client with primary hypothyroidism who takes levothyroxine daily. Which laboratory finding best indicates that the client's current medication dose is adequate?

A) Elevated Thyroid Stimulating Hormone (TSH) level.
B) Low serum calcium level.
C) Thyroid Stimulating Hormone (TSH) level within normal limits.
D) Elevated serum glucose level.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Data Interpretation
**System Focus:** Thyroid medication

In primary hypothyroidism, the pituitary gland produces high amounts of TSH to try to stimulate the failing thyroid gland. When a client is receiving an adequate replacement dose of levothyroxine, the pituitary senses the normal levels of thyroid hormone in the blood and stops overproducing TSH, returning the TSH level to normal limits. An elevated TSH (Option A) would indicate the levothyroxine dose is too low. Calcium (Option B) and glucose (Option D) are not primary indicators used to monitor the adequacy of thyroid hormone replacement therapy.
</details>
### Question 266
**Lens:** Client Teaching
A nurse is reinforcing teaching with a client who is newly prescribed levothyroxine for hypothyroidism. Which statement by the client indicates an accurate understanding of the instructions?

A) "I should take this medication right before I go to sleep at night."
B) "I can stop taking this pill once my daily energy levels return to normal."
C) "I will take this pill in the morning on an empty stomach."
D) "I should take this medication with my breakfast to prevent stomach upset."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens Explanation</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** Levothyroxine is a synthetic thyroid hormone used to treat hypothyroidism. It should be taken in the morning on an empty stomach, at least 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast, to maximize absorption. Taking it with food or at night can decrease its effectiveness. It is a lifelong medication, so the client should not stop taking it when symptoms improve.
**Lens:** *Client Teaching* - This lens evaluates the nurse's ability to ensure the client understands how to safely and effectively take prescribed endocrine medications.
</details>

---

### Question 267
**Lens:** Data Interpretation
A nurse is reviewing the capillary blood glucose levels of a client with type 1 diabetes. The client is sweating, trembling, and reports feeling dizzy. The blood glucose monitor reads 52 mg/dL. How should the nurse interpret these findings?

A) The client is developing diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA).
B) The client is exhibiting signs of hypoglycemia.
C) The client is showing signs of insulin resistance.
D) The client is experiencing the expected dawn phenomenon.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens Explanation</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** A blood glucose level below 70 mg/dL, accompanied by symptoms such as sweating (diaphoresis), trembling, and dizziness, indicates hypoglycemia (low blood sugar). DKA is associated with severe hyperglycemia (high blood sugar). Insulin resistance is a hallmark of type 2 diabetes, not an acute low blood sugar event. The dawn phenomenon refers to early morning hyperglycemia.
**Lens:** *Data Interpretation* - This lens focuses on the nurse's ability to translate objective data (blood glucose 52 mg/dL) and subjective/objective symptoms into a clinical finding (hypoglycemia).
</details>

---

### Question 268
**Lens:** Expected vs Unexpected
A nurse is explaining the basic pathophysiology of type 1 diabetes mellitus to a newly diagnosed adolescent. Which expected characteristic of this condition should the nurse include in the explanation?

A) The pancreas produces no insulin due to autoimmune destruction of beta cells.
B) The body produces insulin, but the tissues are highly resistant to its effects.
C) The condition can usually be managed long-term with diet and oral medications alone.
D) The onset of symptoms typically occurs gradually over several decades.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens Explanation</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** Type 1 diabetes is characterized by an absolute lack of insulin due to the autoimmune destruction of the pancreatic beta cells. Therefore, clients with type 1 diabetes require lifelong exogenous insulin therapy. Insulin resistance, management with oral medications, and a gradual onset of symptoms over decades are all expected characteristics of *type 2* diabetes, not type 1.
**Lens:** *Expected vs Unexpected* - This lens tests the nurse's knowledge of the standard, expected disease process and characteristics of type 1 diabetes versus type 2 diabetes.
</details>

---

### Question 269
**Lens:** Delegation
A client with type 2 diabetes feels shaky and hungry, and their blood glucose is 60 mg/dL. The registered nurse (RN) is currently performing a sterile procedure in another room. Which action is appropriate for the RN to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Assess the client's gag reflex before providing an oral carbohydrate.
B) Administer the prescribed PRN dose of intramuscular glucagon.
C) Determine if the client's symptoms have resolved after eating.
D) Provide the client with 4 ounces of apple juice as directed.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens Explanation</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** Providing a specific, standard carbohydrate snack (like 4 oz of juice) to a conscious client is a task that can be safely delegated to a UAP under RN direction. Assessing reflexes, administering injectable medications (glucagon), and evaluating the client's response to an intervention all require nursing judgment and fall outside the UAP's scope of practice.
**Lens:** *Delegation* - This lens evaluates the nurse's understanding of the scope of practice for assistive personnel when responding to basic hypoglycemia.
</details>

---

### Question 270
**Lens:** Risk Recognition
A nurse is conducting a health screening at a local community center. Which client should the nurse identify as having the highest risk for developing type 2 diabetes mellitus?

A) A 20-year-old who has an autoimmune history of celiac disease.
B) A 45-year-old who exercises daily and has a body mass index (BMI) of 21.
C) A 40-year-old with a body mass index (BMI) of 31 and a sedentary lifestyle.
D) A 12-year-old who recently recovered from a severe viral infection.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens Explanation</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** The primary risk factors for type 2 diabetes include obesity (BMI ≥ 30), advancing age, and physical inactivity (sedentary lifestyle). The 40-year-old with a BMI of 31 who is sedentary fits this profile perfectly. Autoimmune diseases and viral infections are risk factors more closely associated with the onset of type 1 diabetes. A normal BMI (21) and daily exercise drastically reduce the risk of type 2 diabetes.
**Lens:** *Risk Recognition* - This lens tests the nurse's ability to identify predisposing factors and client characteristics that increase the likelihood of developing a specific condition (Type 2 diabetes).
</details>
### Question 271
**Lens:** Client Teaching
**Focus:** Type 1 vs Type 2 Basics

A nurse is providing education to a client newly diagnosed with Type 1 diabetes. The client asks, "Why do I have to take insulin shots when my uncle just takes pills for his diabetes?" Which response by the nurse is the most accurate?

A. "Pills are only used for Type 1 diabetes when the blood sugar is mildly elevated."
B. "Insulin is required for Type 1 diabetes because your body becomes extremely resistant to the insulin it makes."
C. "Type 1 diabetes means your pancreas makes absolutely no insulin, so it must be replaced through injections."
D. "Type 2 diabetes is always treated with insulin first, then transitioned to pills as the pancreas heals."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer: C**

**Rationale:** Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune condition where the beta cells of the pancreas are destroyed, resulting in an absolute lack of insulin production. Therefore, exogenous insulin must be injected to sustain life. Type 2 diabetes is primarily characterized by insulin resistance and relative insulin deficiency, which can often be managed with oral medications (pills) that improve insulin sensitivity or stimulate the pancreas to release more insulin. Options A and D are factually incorrect regarding the disease processes. Option B describes Type 2 diabetes (insulin resistance), not Type 1.
</details>

---

### Question 272
**Lens:** Procedural Step
**Focus:** Hypoglycemia

A client with diabetes reports feeling shaky, dizzy, and sweating profusely. The nurse checks the client's capillary blood glucose level, which reads 58 mg/dL. The client is awake, alert, and able to swallow safely. What is the nurse's immediate next step?

A. Administer exactly 15 grams of a fast-acting simple carbohydrate.
B. Give the client a high-protein snack, such as a handful of almonds or a boiled egg.
C. Administer 1 mg of glucagon intramuscularly into the vastus lateralis.
D. Provide a complex carbohydrate and a protein, such as half a turkey sandwich.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer: A**

**Rationale:** The "Rule of 15" is the standard procedural step for treating a conscious client with hypoglycemia (blood glucose less than 70 mg/dL). The nurse should immediately administer 15 grams of a fast-acting, simple carbohydrate (such as 4 oz of fruit juice or regular soda) to rapidly raise the blood sugar, then recheck the level in 15 minutes. Proteins and complex carbohydrates (Options B and D) digest too slowly to quickly correct acute hypoglycemia. Glucagon (Option C) is reserved for clients who are unconscious, seizing, or unable to safely swallow.
</details>

---

### Question 273
**Lens:** Expected vs Unexpected
**Focus:** Thyroid Medication

A client is taking a daily dose of levothyroxine to treat hypothyroidism. The nurse is evaluating the client's response to the medication. Which of the following assessment findings represents an unexpected, adverse response indicating the medication dose may be too high?

A. Weight loss of 2 pounds over the last month.
B. Increased daily energy levels and decreased feelings of fatigue.
C. Soft, formed, regular daily bowel movements.
D. A resting heart rate of 115 beats per minute and a mild hand tremor.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer: D**

**Rationale:** Levothyroxine is a synthetic thyroid hormone. If the dose is too high, it can induce symptoms of hyperthyroidism (thyrotoxicity), which include an elevated heart rate (tachycardia), palpitations, heat intolerance, and tremors. Therefore, a resting heart rate of 115 bpm is an unexpected, adverse finding that requires the provider to re-evaluate the dose. Options A, B, and C are expected, therapeutic outcomes of treating hypothyroidism, as the medication restores a normal metabolic rate, improves energy, and resolves constipation.
</details>

---

### Question 274
**Lens:** Delegation
**Focus:** Hypoglycemia

The registered nurse (RN) is caring for a client with Type 2 diabetes who has a history of sudden hypoglycemic episodes. Which task is appropriate for the RN to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A. Assessing the client's skin for cold, clammy diaphoresis.
B. Performing a point-of-care capillary blood glucose check before meals.
C. Educating the client on the early warning signs of low blood sugar.
D. Determining if the client needs 15 grams of juice or an intramuscular glucagon injection.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer: B**

**Rationale:** Unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) are trained to perform standard, routine tasks with predictable outcomes, such as obtaining a point-of-care capillary blood glucose reading. Assessing skin for signs of hypoglycemia (Option A), educating the client (Option C), and making clinical judgments about which medication or intervention to administer (Option D) all fall under the RN scope of practice and cannot be delegated. 
</details>

---

### Question 275
**Lens:** Data Interpretation
**Focus:** Hypoglycemia

The nurse is reviewing the morning laboratory and point-of-care test results for a client with Type 1 diabetes. Based on basic endocrine parameters, which specific finding indicates an acute hypoglycemic event requiring immediate nursing intervention?

A. A 2-hour postprandial blood glucose of 130 mg/dL.
B. A fasting blood glucose level of 95 mg/dL.
C. A hemoglobin A1C level of 6.8%.
D. A capillary blood glucose reading of 48 mg/dL.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer: D**

**Rationale:** Hypoglycemia is clinically defined as a blood glucose level below 70 mg/dL. A capillary reading of 48 mg/dL requires immediate intervention with fast-acting carbohydrates. A fasting blood glucose of 95 mg/dL (Option B) and a 2-hour postprandial reading of 130 mg/dL (Option A) are both well within normal, safe target ranges for a diabetic client. An A1C of 6.8% (Option C) indicates good long-term glycemic control (typically aiming for less than 7% in diabetic clients) and is a historical marker, not an acute emergency.
</details>
### Question 276
**Lens:** Data Interpretation  
A nurse is reviewing the point-of-care blood glucose reading for a client with Type 1 diabetes. The reading is 58 mg/dL. Based on this data, which physical finding should the nurse expect to observe?

A. Hot, dry skin  
B. Profuse sweating  
C. Deep, rapid respirations  
D. Fruity breath odor  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Details</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> B. Profuse sweating  
<strong>Rationale:</strong> A blood glucose level of 58 mg/dL indicates hypoglycemia (low blood sugar). Common expected signs of hypoglycemia include profuse sweating (diaphoresis), shakiness, tachycardia, and confusion. Hot, dry skin, deep rapid respirations (Kussmaul breathing), and fruity breath odor are classic signs of hyperglycemia and diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA), not hypoglycemia.  
</details>

---

### Question 277
**Lens:** Client Teaching  
A nurse is providing discharge instructions to a client newly prescribed levothyroxine for hypothyroidism. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the teaching?

A. "I should take this medication right before I go to sleep."  
B. "I will take my pill with a large glass of milk during breakfast."  
C. "I need to take this medication in the morning on an empty stomach."  
D. "I can stop taking this medication once my fatigue goes away."  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Details</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C. "I need to take this medication in the morning on an empty stomach."  
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Levothyroxine must be taken on an empty stomach, typically 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast, to ensure optimal absorption. Taking it with food or milk impairs absorption. It is usually a lifelong medication, so the client should not stop taking it even if symptoms improve. Taking it at bedtime can cause insomnia.  
</details>

---

### Question 278
**Lens:** Therapeutic Communication  
A young adult client newly diagnosed with Type 1 diabetes asks the nurse, "Why do I have to take shots? My grandfather has diabetes and he only takes pills." Which is the most appropriate therapeutic response by the nurse?

A. "Your body does not make insulin anymore, so you must receive it through injections."  
B. "Pills are only used for older adults who have had diabetes for many years."  
C. "Your grandfather has a more severe type of diabetes than you do."  
D. "You will only need shots for a few months until your pancreas heals."  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Details</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A. "Your body does not make insulin anymore, so you must receive it through injections."  
<strong>Rationale:</strong> This response provides a clear, factual, and easy-to-understand explanation of the fundamental difference between Type 1 and Type 2 diabetes. Type 1 diabetes involves the autoimmune destruction of pancreatic beta cells, resulting in an absolute lack of insulin, which necessitates lifelong insulin injections. Type 2 diabetes involves insulin resistance and is often managed with oral medications (pills). Type 1 is not a temporary condition, and age is not the determining factor for taking pills.  
</details>

---

### Question 279
**Lens:** Procedural Step  
A conscious, alert client with Type 2 diabetes reports feeling shaky and hungry. The nurse checks the client's blood glucose level, which is 62 mg/dL. Which procedural step should the nurse take next to treat this client?

A. Administer 50% dextrose intravenously.  
B. Give the client a dose of rapid-acting insulin.  
C. Instruct the client to eat a full, heavy meal immediately.  
D. Provide 4 ounces of regular apple juice.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Details</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> D. Provide 4 ounces of regular apple juice.  
<strong>Rationale:</strong> The client is experiencing mild hypoglycemia and is conscious and alert. The correct procedural step is to follow the "Rule of 15" by providing 15 grams of fast-acting carbohydrates, such as 4 ounces of regular (non-diet) fruit juice or soda. Giving insulin would dangerously lower the blood sugar further. IV dextrose is reserved for unconscious or severe hypoglycemic patients. A heavy meal takes too long to digest to treat immediate hypoglycemia.  
</details>

---

### Question 280
**Lens:** Evaluation  
A nurse is evaluating the effectiveness of levothyroxine therapy for a client diagnosed with hypothyroidism six weeks ago. Which client finding indicates that the medication is having the desired effect?

A. The client complains of feeling cold all the time.  
B. The client's resting heart rate is 115 beats per minute.  
C. The client reports improved energy levels and wakefulness.  
D. The client has experienced an unintentional weight gain of 6 lbs.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Details</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C. The client reports improved energy levels and wakefulness.  
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Hypothyroidism slows the body's metabolism, causing symptoms like profound fatigue, weight gain, and cold intolerance. An effective response to levothyroxine (thyroid hormone replacement) would reverse these symptoms, leading to improved energy levels. Continued cold intolerance and weight gain indicate the dose may be too low. A heart rate of 115 bpm (tachycardia) may indicate the dose is too high, pushing the client into hyperthyroidism.  
</details>


# SECTION: Respiratory System (Count: 140 Questions)


### Question 281
A nurse is providing discharge education to a client who will be using continuous home oxygen therapy. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the teaching?

A. "I will apply petroleum jelly to my nose if it becomes dry from the oxygen."
B. "I will wear cotton clothing instead of synthetic fabrics while using my oxygen."
C. "I can occasionally smoke in the room as long as the oxygen machine is turned off."
D. "I will keep my oxygen tank right next to my space heater to keep the tubing warm."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** 
**Client Teaching:** Cotton clothing should be worn instead of synthetic fabrics (like nylon or polyester) because synthetics can generate static electricity, which poses a fire risk in an oxygen-rich environment. Petroleum jelly is a combustible substance and should not be used; water-soluble lubricants are recommended instead. Clients must never smoke near oxygen, even if turned off, as oxygen saturates the environment and clothing. Oxygen tanks must be kept well away from heat sources like space heaters or open flames to prevent explosions and fires.
</details>

---

### Question 282
A nurse is caring for a client with bacterial pneumonia who has been placed on droplet precautions. Which task is appropriate for the nurse to delegate to Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP)?

A. Teaching the client how to properly use an incentive spirometer.
B. Assessing the client's lung sounds for improvement or worsening of crackles.
C. Restocking the personal protective equipment (PPE) cart located outside the client's room.
D. Explaining the purpose of droplet precautions to the client's visiting family members.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** 
**Delegation:** Restocking supplies, such as a PPE cart, is a standard, non-clinical task that falls safely within the scope of practice for Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP). Teaching, assessing, and explaining complex medical precautions require nursing judgment and clinical knowledge, and therefore must be performed by the registered nurse. 
</details>

---

### Question 283
The nurse is instructing a client on how to properly use a metered-dose inhaler (MDI) without a spacer. What is the correct first step in this procedure?

A. Exhale completely to empty the lungs before bringing the inhaler to the mouth.
B. Press down firmly on the canister while inhaling rapidly.
C. Remove the mouthpiece cap and shake the inhaler vigorously for several seconds.
D. Hold the breath for 10 seconds after inhaling the medication.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** 
**Procedural Step:** The very first step when preparing to use a metered-dose inhaler is to remove the cap and shake the inhaler well (usually 5 to 6 times). This ensures the medication and propellant are properly mixed before administration. Exhaling completely is done right before taking the breath in. Inhaling should be slow and deep, not rapid. Holding the breath is a later step performed after the medication has been inhaled.
</details>

---

### Question 284
A nurse enters the room of a client receiving continuous oxygen therapy via nasal cannula. Which observation places the client at the highest risk for injury?

A. An electric heating pad is placed on the client's lower back.
B. The client is drinking water from a plastic cup with a straw.
C. The oxygen flow rate is set continuously at 2 L/min.
D. A large "No Smoking" sign is taped securely to the room door.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** 
**Risk Recognition:** Oxygen supports combustion, making fires burn hotter and faster. Electrical devices, such as electric heating pads, electric razors, or radios, can create a spark that could easily ignite a fire in an oxygen-rich environment. Therefore, the use of an electric heating pad poses a significant safety risk.
